#6897. We Spent Two Years Testing Mattresses—These Are the Best for Back Pain

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

We found pressure-relieving picks for every sleep style and budget.

A great mattress turns your bedtime routine into the best part of your day. But a subpar mattress will make you dread turning in. We all deserve somewhere to rest that eases our aches and pains. After over two years of testing, the editors of House Beautiful are ready to reveal the best mattresses for back pain on the market.

We spent at least six months sleeping on each mattress below, evaluating their firmness, support, and relief on the pressure points. Every editor has a different sleep need: couples prioritize motion isolation, while stomach sleepers need enough firmness to keep their spine aligned.

Now, you may be thinking that mattresses designed to aid in joint support are going to be extremely expensive, and we won't lie to you; some of the mattresses on our list are pricey (the most costly one is just over $3,000). However, three of our top picks are well under $1,000, so if you want to save while still looking out for your back, you've come to the right place. Otherwise, feel free to splurge because these luxury mattresses are well worth it. And most of our picks come with a lengthy trial period, so you can take your time making sure it’s right for you.

Trust us; if you haven't been sleeping very well for more than a few weeks, your mattress may be to blame. Scroll through our list, read our honest thoughts on these mattresses, and add your favorite one to your cart.

Although Saatva's Rx mattress is "designed for sleepers with chronic back and joint conditions," the comfort level creates a dream sleep experience for all. Just ask tester Katelyn Lunders, who is a firm believer in the mattress after sleeping comfortably night after night for the past two years.

"As a combination sleeper with sciatica, I have found the Rx incredibly supportive and comfortable, whether I sleep on my back or side," she says. "Thanks to the mattress's seven (yes, seven) layers, the firmness isn't one note, like my former memory foam mattress. What sets the Rx apart from other mattresses on the market is its additional layers of pressure-relieving support. The Rx offers more support in the center third of the mattress to help align your spine while you sleep and has a gel-infused memory foam layer to support your lower back. Each section of the mattress works together to relieve pressure points and create a feeling of being cradled, which I love.

I currently sleep alone, so I get out to sprawl out across my Rx, but when I have shared the bed, I have experienced no issues with the edge support. Unlike some mattresses that will roll you off as you attempt to get out of bed in the morning, the Saatva Rx holds steady, thanks to firm spring coils around the mattress's perimeter. The coils provide support without sacrificing comfort due to the layer of foam on top of them.”

When you think of a mattress designed to soothe joint and back pain, you may picture a hefty price tag attached, but this Zinus find is the one exception. At only $230 (for a queen-size), this pick gets the job done for a fraction of the price. Just ask Lunders, who says, “I was personally so happy with my bed-in-a-box memory foam mattress that I told anyone who would listen to buy one, including my own family, who share my love of a good deal.” As for the back pain component, a firm mattress will do wonders, and Lunders notes that this mattress is definitely not soft. “I slept on my Zinus mattress for five years, and it never sagged. If anything, it became more comfortable with time,” she adds.

One more thing about the pressure relief component: Zinus enlisted its patented zoned pressure relief technology designed to help reduce even the smallest aches and pains that may arise throughout the night.

Not everyone has the budget for a luxury mattress that costs thousands of dollars, so if you want an under-$500 mattress that is supportive and holds up well over time, check out contributing writer Kelsey Mulvey's pick.

“As far as I’m concerned, Allswell’s Luxe Hybrid Mattress offers the best bang for your buck,” Mulvey says. “I mean, the King mattress I have costs under $500! It’s sometimes hard to find bedding for that price point! Layers of foam—think a copper-infused memory foam and a shock-absorbing high-density support foam—are paired with an eight-inch coil unit to create the perfect medium-firm base. It’s soft and cloud-like yet provides the right amount of structure, which my husband and I really appreciate.”

“We first got this mattress back in 2019, and it has endured a pandemic, a move, an engagement, and many lazy nights watching movies in bed. While we have experimented with different mattress pads and bed frames, this pick has stayed in tip-top shape and has no obvious sags or dips.”

Tester Samantha Jones was waking up with shoulder and back pain most mornings. But when she tested out the cooling Leesa Sapira Hybrid mattress, that changed.

“Its squishy memory foam layers on top provide a sink-in feeling like you're climbing into a bed at a luxury hotel,” Jones says. “The individually wrapped coils below did their job of providing support for my hips, shoulders, and back, making the bed comfy yet not like quicksand. Notoriously someone who tosses and turns for hours before falling asleep, I've actually found that I drift off to sleep faster on the Sapira Hybrid than my previous, firmer mattress model.”

“I have been waking up with significantly less back pain on the Sapira Hybrid. As an avid runner, I'm accustomed to waking up with tight muscles after a workout. But because the individually wrapped coils contour to my body and provide adequate support for my hips at night, I've noticed less lower back pain and stiffness. The same is true for my shoulder pain: The balance of squish and support has helped eliminate shoulder discomfort.”

Senior Commerce Editor Marina Liao’s customizable Naturepedic mattress allowed her to build a bespoke sleep routine. “I initially slept on the cushion-firm layers and liked them, but after a few weeks, I noticed they didn’t feel as comfy, especially when my sciatica flared up,” Liao says. “The layers suddenly felt too soft for me. Naturepedic offers a 100-night sleep trial, which gives you ample time to test the mattress thoroughly. I switched my layers for the ‘extra firm’ option and immediately liked their feel much better.”

“The mattress feels soft when you’re lying on it, and though you might intuitively think this means you’ll wake up with an achy back, that hasn’t been my experience. The layers ensure you feel supported from the top of your head to your neck, spine, and even hips. I’ll also add that this mattress has a 12-inch height, so it is taller than my old mattress, which makes me feel elevated off the ground and like I’m sleeping on a cloud.”

As a stomach sleeper, Social Media Editor Bianca Bonetti is used to waking up with back pain. She sleeps comfortably since switching to the pillow-top Estate, and to her, it feels like every pressure point is supported.

“Considering I sleep in a position that is bad for my back, it’s kind of a miracle that I haven’t woken up with soreness since sleeping on the Estate mattress,” Bonetti says. "Unless it’s the day after a tough workout. I was hoping to get the best of both worlds by still feeling cocooned and like I’m sleeping on a cloud, but looking out for the health of my back and posture,” Bonetti says. “I love that I get the low back support I need without sinking into the mattress. I do think I would have purchased a medium pillow top if it had been an option for the full size, but I was pleasantly surprised by my love for a firm mattress.”

Shopping Editor Jessica Dukes is pretty picky about her bed and bedding. Comfort and support have to come first, as she has sciatica that doesn’t respond well to poofy mattresses. So the Casper Dream was her ideal switch.

“Night after night, I have found this mattress to be extremely comfortable,” Dukes says. “I also didn't notice too much of a difference between how it felt the first night and how it feels several weeks in, so if you don't want to wait up to 48 hours to start using it, I think that's okay. Despite my back issues, the medium firmness of the memory foam is a delight, which is high praise considering how picky I am. The mattress gives me a slight ‘sinking in’ feeling, but it's supportive at the same time. The result is balanced comfort, and whether I fall asleep on my side or back, I wake up with zero back pain. As for my husband's tossing and turning, I barely feel him move, as those coils do a great job of minimizing the effects.”

If you've ever seen a video of someone dropping an uncooked egg onto a purple grid and being amazed that the egg didn't immediately explode, you're familiar with the mattress brand Purple. That famous purple grid lives beneath the surface of most of its products, as it's the backbone (pun intended) of its creations. The grid in question is what gives you that cradled support ideal for anyone who likes a little extra TLC on their backs. “Given that I weigh much more than an egg, I was skeptical. But after a year of sleeping on the mattress, I'm officially a convert,” says Associate Shopping Editor Janae McKenzie.

“Just above the supportive coils is the purple GelFlex grid, the feature Purple is best known for. The Premier has the thickest grid, so it gives the softest hold,” McKenzie adds. “It's the first mattress I've tested that lives up to its promise to cradle me. Plus, the isolated coils provide pinpoint relief, almost like the mattress can sense where I need extra support and deliver it.” With Purple, you don't really have to settle for a firm mattress (if that's not your thing) to protect your back.

There may not be white-glove delivery involved for the Nectar mattress, which comes in a box and unrolls in 24 hours for you to sleep on, but it feels just as nice as some of the other luxury models on the market. "I grew up sleeping on rock-hard mattresses, so I felt dubious about foam options, thinking they would feel too soft and hurt my back. My Nectar has completely changed my mind about this. Now I can't even go back to sleeping on the surfaces I've previously slept on," Liao says. Nectar offers its mattress in three different tiers with various features: Nectar, Nectar Premier, and Nectar Premier Cooper, which you can read all about on the website. For a brief overview, though, the models differ in height and offer relief for different types of sleepers.

"We opted for just the Nectar in a queen size, which is a 12-inch mattress with cooling capabilities," Liao says. "I would love to take credit for finding the mattress, but it was actually my husband who bought it six years ago while we were still dating for his apartment. I asked him why he bought it, and he said, 'It had good reviews on Reddit or something. I don't remember, but I love it.' I have to concur because it does check the boxes that are important to us, which are comfort and support, as both of us have sciatica.

The mattress is indeed medium-firm, and even after countless nights of rest, it has maintained this feel. When we get up to use the bathroom at night, neither of us feels the other waking up, which speaks to the multiple layers of the Nectar that minimize motion transfer. Both of us have experienced periodic night sweats on this mattress (when our AC breaks or when we're stressed about work), so it's not as cooling as the description says, but with the right sheets (linen!), it does feel cooler. However, if you run extra hot, you may want to upgrade to the Nectar Premier Copper, which has triple-action cooling power.I really love my Nectar, and when it comes time to change it, I'll just buy a Nectar again. In fact, several people who have slept on this bed actually ended up purchasing it right after, including my husband's cousin, who was then pregnant with her third child. She immediately bought it for her and her husband after napping on it!"

How We Selected the Most Comfortable Mattresses

As you might've guessed, the phrase "most comfortable" is subjective when you're talking about a mattress. We all have different sleep preferences, so there isn't a one-size-fits-all approach to shopping for one. In order to know which mattresses are actually comfy and which are not, we started by asking our in-house experts: House Beautiful's very own editors. Many of us test beds for a living (it's part of our jobs), so we only recommend the ones we truly stand by. Of course, in our research, some editors candidly said they don't like their mattresses, so you won't find those named on our best-of list.

After we had our pool of editors who love their mattresses, we made sure everyone had slept on theirs for at least six months. You can only tell if the mattress is truly comfortable over a period of time by sleeping on it every day, which is why plenty of the brands on our list have long sleep trial periods. Additionally, you can rest assured that everyone on the House Beautiful team has different sleeping habits, which translates nicely to a well-rounded curation. You'll find comfortable mattresses for those with back pain, comfortable mattresses for side sleepers, and even comfortable mattresses for the whole family (pets included!). Every editor also provided pros and cons for their mattresses, so you have all the details to make the most informed purchase decision.

What to Look for in the Most Comfortable Mattress

There are several qualities to take note of when you're shopping for a comfortable mattress. They are:

Mattress Type: You will notice that the mattresses on our list are either foam or a hybrid. Foam mattresses are made of memory foam and other layers of foam without any springs or internal structures. Meanwhile, hybrid mattresses typically have coils or an innerspring along with a comfortable layer on top. This layer can be made from various materials, from polyfoam or latex to memory foam. One type isn't better than the other since personal preference will play a role here.

Memory foam is known for conforming to your body's shape and cradling you while providing support. You don't have to worry about squeaky springs (there are none), and the dense foam layers make it hypoallergenic because dust and mites will find it hard to penetrate the deep layers. Some drawbacks of memory foam, though, include trapping heat while you sleep and less edge support. This means you sink in if you sit on the edge of the mattress since memory foam doesn't have coils for support.

On the flip side, since a hybrid has a coil support system, it will feel sturdy and supportive. There's less chance of your mattress sagging over time, and hybrids can support heavier weights. The coils, too, allow for more airflow, so your mattress holds in less heat. Some downsides to hybrids: they are often more expensive than foam mattresses, and they are usually heavier to maneuver.

Firmness: Everyone has a different tolerance for firmness; some prefer a softer feel, while others like a rock-hard mattress. Firmness is calculated by how far one sinks into a mattress. And remember: firmness does not equate to support. The latter is determined by how the mattress helps a sleeper maintain spinal alignment. So, you can have a firm bed but not have it be supportive for your back. To test for support in person, lie on the mattress for at least 10 to 15 minutes—the mattress should push back slightly to support your body. You will know if the mattress supports your body or not after sleeping on it for a few nights. If you get aches and pains anywhere, that means the mattress is not supportive.

Temperature Regulation: If you're a hot sleeper, you know how important it is to find a mattress that is more cooling, which often comes in the form of a hybrid model. (The coils don't trap as much heat as multiple layers of an all-foam mattress.) Look for a mattress with elements like a breathable cover made of cotton or natural latex that regulates body temperature. Mattresses with gel-infused memory foam are also good indicators that they'll cool you as you sleep.

Motion Isolation: If you sleep alone, this probably isn't important to you, but if you sleep with a partner or pet, pay attention to this category. A mattress that can reduce motion transfer will help you get a good night's rest, whether your partner is getting out of bed early for a workout (and you want to sleep in) or your pet is jumping on/off the bed throughout the night. All the picks on our list have excellent motion isolation, but a simple test you can do at home is to put a cup of water on the bed. Press your hands down on one side or even dramatically plop onto the bed—if the glass of water doesn't spill or the liquid barely moves in the cup, the mattress has good motion isolation.

Trial Period: Almost all mattress brands will offer a lengthy trial period for testing out your new piece. You can't sleep on a mattress for a week and expect it to still feel the same months down the line! We love brands that offer a year-long trial, but chances are, when you buy a mattress and it works, you likely won't go through the hassle of returning it (unless it is really terrible!). Make sure to check the return policy and how to return your mattress if needed, as this can affect whether or not you want to buy it.

What to Consider When Buying the Most Comfortable Mattress

Don't forget these personal preferences when shopping for a mattress. They will play a role in what you end up buying.

Budget: When buying any big piece of furniture, price is always a concern. You may not have the budget to spend thousands of dollars on a comfy mattress, but that doesn't mean you should sacrifice your sleep for a more inferior model. Thankfully, our editors all had different budgeting constraints in mind when buying mattresses, so our list contains comfy mattresses that give you the most for your buck to more luxurious options that make you feel like you're staying at a five-star hotel. You can find a quality and supportive mattress for under $500!

Sleeping Position: Now that you're familiar with the different types of mattresses, what is your sleep position? If you're a side sleeper, for instance, memory foam and hybrids are good for pressure-relieving comfort. If you're a heavier sleeper, you may want to opt for a hybrid since the coils will provide more support. If you like your mattress to feel very stiff, then an all-foam option may not be the best for you. Stomach sleepers, like one of our editors, will need medium-firm to firm support, which foam and hybrid will provide. We highlighted the comfiest mattresses for the different types of sleepers above, but again, you won't know until you try one out for yourself. Make sure to read through our notes and the product description, as a good brand will note whether or not this is good for all types of sleeping positions or just caters to one.

Body Type: Similar to how your height affects the type of mattress you buy (you don't want your feet dangling off!), your weight will also factor in the purchase decision. Sleepers who weigh 130 pounds or less can handle a softer mattress because they don't sink too deep into the mattress. If you weigh between 130 and 230 pounds, go for a medium-soft to medium-firm mattress. For those who weigh more than 230 pounds, a firmer mattress with extra support is ideal.

How to Pick the Most Comfortable Mattresses for Pain

Chances are, if you're looking for a comfortable mattress, it's because you've been sleeping on a mattress that gives you back pain, or you have a pre-existing condition that makes picking a mattress trickier. Here, we break down what to look for if you have:

Back pain: Look for a mattress with a medium-firm to firm surface. Studies show that this might effectively reduce back pain in individuals. Mattresses like the ones from Saatva even spotlight that they're good for people with back pain and are chiropractor-approved. Look for these types of callouts in your mattress as you shop.

Hip pain: The best mattresses for hip pain offer firm lumbar support with gentle pressure relief. The mattresses should have a supportive edge and be high enough to allow your hips to remain above your knees when you're sitting on them. Memory foam is often good for hip pain as it contours to your body, but keep in mind that if you're a stomach or back sleeper with hip pain, this material might allow too much sinkage, and your spine won't be properly aligned—going with a hybrid model then is more beneficial.

Shoulder pain: For people with shoulder pain, we didn't forget about you! Look for memory foam or latex mattresses that will provide pressure relief and support as they contour to your body the best. In particular, if you're a side sleeper with shoulder pain, opt for a softer mattress vs. a firmer one. Also, consider switching out your pillow, as the wrong one will misalign your spine, causing both neck and shoulder pain. Shop our best pillows for neck pains.

House Beautiful editors are experts in all things bedding, from having written about the softest sheets to reviewing the most luxurious mattresses. Our editors have tested and thoroughly reviewed popular mattresses from the likes of Nolah, Saatva, Purple, and more, plus traded notes with one another so we can recommend the best options to readers. For this story on comfortable mattresses, since the topic is entirely dependent on personal preferences, we asked our fellow editors for their top-rated picks that they themselves own and have slept on for months to years at a time. There is no better indicator that a mattress is comfortable if you've had it for a long time, and it helps alleviate any pain! We spent over a week making sure the list was packed with a variety of brands and mattress types so readers could find the one that most closely matches their own personal preferences. Every mattress on this list is thoroughly vetted by the team.

#6896. We Found the Ultimate Cold-Weather Cologne

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Fulton & Roark's new scent, inspired by a church in Gary, Indiana, is phenomenal.

Welcome to the Esquire Endorsement. Heavily researched. Thoroughly vetted. These picks are the best way to spend your hard-earned cash.

Halloween is in the rearview, Daylight Saving Time is over, and the sun has decided it has better places to be (although I am grateful for that extra hour of sleep we got). You know what all that means: It’s officially time to bring out the poofy coats, the cable-knit sweaters, and the winter scents. We’re talking spicy, smoky, sophisticated, and a little dark. Think of it as the olfactory equivalent of a cashmere scarf or quilted jacket. But before you reach for the same old colognes of winters past, consider making room for something new in your fragrance rotation.

Allow me to suggest my new cold-weather go-to, released just last month by one of my all-time favorite niche fragrance houses, Fulton & Roark. This extrait has a pretty sick name: Cathedral of Ruins—and it smells every bit as atmospheric as you’d hope for with a name like that. It’s moody without being gloomy, warm without being cloying, and oh-so intriguing. Put a spritz of this stuff on and you’re guaranteed to start hearing the words, What are you wearing?

Fulton & Roark does what it calls “American fine fragrance,” meaning it doesn’t follow all the rules of your classic European perfumery. The perfumer looks to new ingredients, formulation methods, and scent combinations for all of its fragrances. Oh, and every fragrance is unisex, truly unisex. My boyfriend loves the scents as much as I do, and we often share them.

And since this is American fine fragrance we’re talking about, it only makes sense that each of the brand’s fragrances are named after and inspired by a real American place with a story behind it. That rule even holds for the brand’s name: Fulton is for Fulton County, Georgia; and Roark is for Roark’s Cove in Sewanee, Tennessee. So where is this Cathedral of Ruins? Gary, Indiana. Birthplace of the Jackson 5, and our clearest example of Rust Belt deindustrialization. The once-booming steel city is now also home to thousands of abandoned and decaying buildings, including the City Methodist Church—once one of the grandest churches in the Midwest.

Fulton and Roark co-founder Kevin Keller showed me photos of what City Methodist Church looks like now, which he took on his own trip to Gary, and the immediate sense I got from these photos was a devastating sort of beauty (or a beautiful sort of devastation). The church’s roof has fallen in; the only living congregation is the vegetation that reclaims more and more of the space every year. But, its Neo-Gothic stone walls still stand. It’s a dead relic, but it is not a dead space. It pulls up mixed feelings. This Cathedral of Ruins extrait de parfum is Fulton & Roark’s homage to it, and it nails everything you get from seeing a picture of the place.

Like I said, Cathedral of Ruins is the perfect cold-weather scent. But instead of the typical rich, gourmand notes you get in fall and winter fragrances like vanilla, caramel, and amber, Cathedral of Ruins relies on spices, smoke, and woods to create a warm atmosphere. This is not a syrupy sweet winter scent—it’s smoldering, Gothic-inspired, sophisticated, and unlike any scent you’ve ever smelled before.

At the top, you get resinous cypress, spicy black peppercorn, and, unexpectedly, floral rose. The lush, velvety notes of rose lead into the more delicate floral notes of saffron, symbolizing the grace of the cathedral (and possibly the new natural growth of vegetation over the ruins). Rich Peru balsam finishes out the heart with more warm, woody vibes.

But the base is where this fragrance really stands out. The star of the show here is burned frankincense, the classic scent of sacred ritual—imagine smoke rising above the walls, past the ceiling that’s no longer there. Frankincense is, obviously, smoky, but not in the same way as something like tobacco; the frankincense here is dry, resinous, and subtly fresh like pine. To me, frankincense even has a waxy-green quality similar to olives. The warm depth of leathered woods and musks finish out the fragrance, leaving the intrigue of smoky woods to linger on your skin.

#6893. This Unexpected Antique Is Making a Major Comeback—and It Might Be Hiding in Your Grandma’s Art Collection

This centuries-old style brings fresh character to the walls.

Vintage art adds soul to a space. There’s just something about that story and patina that lends itself to more personality for your walls. While oil paintings and tiny art have certainly had their time to shine, there’s another antique art form that’s taking center stage: They’re called reverse glass paintings.

Popular in India in the 18th and 19th centuries, reverse glass paintings are illustrations that have been hand-painted onto the back of glass, giving them a painterly, colorful look. Annalise Holasz, founder of vintage art gallery Hue + Holasz, says that these works have a “really naïve” quality since each is painted by hand, and the “little variations make each one special.”

Holasz also notes that their frames, often made from reclaimed wood, are particularly special, since they provide a charming, aged, and even battered appearance. These artworks can showcase visuals like flowers, plants, Indian princes, boats, and shells, as Holasz says, “but animals are probably the most common,” and include birds, fish, sea creatures, and jungle animals.

To learn more about reverse glass paintings, we tapped the experts, who are sharing what to look for when starting your own collection.

Alexander Widener, owner of Widener Company, believes that reverse glass paintings are rising in popularity because “they capture the spirit of craft and yesteryear that so many of us are craving in our increasingly digital world.” It also doesn’t hurt that earlier this year, designer Tory Burch gave Vogue a tour of her Paris apartment, where reverse glass paintings were spotlighted in her powder room.

“I think people are drawn to how fun and playful they are,” Holasz says. “There’s often a subject that feels personal, and the combination of vibrant color and a characterful wooden frame appeals.”

Melanie Lissack of Melanie Lissack Interiors believes that reverse glass paintings are trending because they are eclectic, unique, and vintage in style while still having a modern twist.

While current-day pieces can look remarkably similar to the bygone versions, Lissack thinks they just don’t carry the same charm as the antique pieces—as striking as they are. Those in the know don’t look at the artwork first; they look at the frame. For instance, Lissack is on the lookout for slightly worn frames.

Widener points out that if you’re questing after particularly old reverse glass paintings, you should look for bubbles in the glass, a sign that the glass was handblown. This was a standard practice up until the early 20th century.

Also, it can be as simple as buying the motifs you love. For Widener, it’s country scenes and images of animals. He’s also not put off if they don’t have a frame at all.

“If you aren’t heading to Jaipur anytime soon, then Etsy and eBay are great places to look,” Holasz shares. “Sellers restock weekly. I’d browse quite a few listings before choosing, because the frame can make such a difference. They can sell pretty quicky as they are always in demand though, so it’s worth doing some research to shortlist styles you like so you can move quickly when the right thing comes along.”

If you’re searching for the truly antique varieties (made in the 18th and 19th centuries), Holasz adds that these are likely more available at auctions.

Also, don’t count out flea markets and antique shops for these finds. For reverse glass paintings, according to current eBay listings, you can expect to pay as low as $5 all the way up to $5,000. The prices for these paintings tend to be a steal these days since they’re just starting to have a moment, so snatch them up before the prices rise.

While you can certainly give the spotlight to a single reverse glass painting, many designers believe these diminutive pieces look best when hung together in small groups.

As for rooms where these works look their best, Holasz thinks they look “really adorable” in children’s rooms, from nurseries to bedrooms to playrooms. They also act as hardy artwork that can be displayed in kitchens and baths, since, as Lissack says, the paintings are “protected by being on the rear side of the glass.”

Also, don’t be afraid to go a bit rogue with these pieces. For example, Holasz likes to mix them into a gallery wall alongside oil paintings, drawings, and sketches, since the different surfaces create visual interest. And you don’t have to hang them on the wall—they also look great leaned on bookshelves.

Widener, who often scores unframed versions, prefers to display them on wire plate hangers.

“When placed in a window, the light comes through in a really beautiful way,” he says.

#6889. The Fringe Pieces Fashion Insiders Are Wearing This Fall

Runways say this is the adornment of the season.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Fringe never really goes out of style. No matter the season, you can always expect to see the embellishment swaying from the arm of a suede jacket or dangling off a leather bag. So when it comes to trends around the design detail, it’s a mere matter of what form the fringe takes. Lately, fashion’s found itself in a fringe fixation, prompting the trim to move beyond the Western-inspired looks it’s best known for.

Fringe re-emerged on the scene last fall when spring/summer 2026 catwalks from Bottega Veneta, Givenchy, and Chanel showcased the adornment as playful ornamentation for skirts, bags, and even shoes. By the fall/winter 2026 season, others were beginning to chime in with their own takes. There were the boho-inspired bags from Etro, the romantic wispy dresses from Ulla Johnson, and restrained silhouettes from Altuzarra.

Whether you’re looking for a bold statement or a little wink of style, fringe remains a simple way to add flair to any outfit. And right now, there’s a fringed version of just about any wardrobe piece you could think of. Between the classic leather staples and newer avant-garde styles, you’ll find something for every style sensibility.

Do an online search for fringe jackets, and you’ll find no shortage of options to shop. Leather and suede remain tried-and-true materials for this style of outerwear—but now there’s also a plethora of other textiles to shop as well. Opt for Toteme’s viral scarf coat if you’re in the market for a new winter coat, or this tweed number from Etro if you’re looking for a polished piece you can wear to the office.

Nili Lotan Eloi Fringe-Trimmed Suede Jacket

Simon Miller Mabel Vegan Leather Fringe Jacket

If recent fall/winter 2026 runways from Givenchy and Proenza Schouler show us anything, it’s that fringe dresses are reaching new heights. The trim is an especially pretty touch to formalwear and event dresses, providing playful movement with every step to even the simplest silhouettes.

Cara Cara Troy Floral-Print Fringe-Trimmed Cotton-Blend Jersey Midi Dress

Ciebon Nella Asymmetric Fringe Midi Dress

Cult Gaia Noosha Fringed Silk Halterneck Maxi Dress

Just like dresses, fringe skirts are experiencing a similarly sophisticated evolution. Lately, the style has favored sleek satin fringe to create a distinct piece that will immediately elevate anything it’s paired with.

Bottega Veneta Fringed Knitted Silk Midi Skirt

The sky’s the limit when it comes to fringe bags these days. There’s long fringe and short fringe. There’s standard leather and suede trim that adds a timeless flair, or more dramatic confections made of sequins, beads, or feathers. With beaded tassel bags on the rise, it makes sense that fringe would take similar cues for an eclectic flourish.

These Boudoir mules, as they came to be known, introduced a playful take on fringe footwear. Now it’s an ultra-luxe, fashion-forward style that’s perfect for those who never shy away from making a statement.

Finding your signature scent involves a lot of trial and error—and a tingling nose. Fragrance is powerful, and discovering a unique scent for daily or occasional wear can uplift your mood, evoke a memory, or make your presence known in any room before you even speak. Whether you’re hunting for a fragrance that feels distinctly you or building a perfume wardrobe to ensure you’re prepared for every event on your social calendar, shopping for perfume can feel overwhelming. One moment, everyone’s going for guava; the next, it’s all about sea salt.

Truth is, trends come and go. However, the journey to building a unique collection is about finding a fragrance that truly resonates—whether floral, gourmand, musky, or a distinct layered blend.

Don’t be afraid to layer a couple of scents for an irreplicable fragrance of your own creation.

Joan Tanis is the national training and events director at Maison Francis Kurkdjian.

Tynan Sinks is a New York City-based beauty writer and the co-host of fragrance podcast Smell Ya Later.

Bérénice Watteau is a fine fragrance perfumer at DSM-Firmenich

What should you consider when choosing a perfume?

Understanding fragrance notes is one of the most effective ways to find your perfect scent. “Very similar to the fashion industry, there can be trends with fragrances as well,” Tanis says. “Choosing a fragrance isn’t always easy, but make the right choice by selecting one that reflects who you are and makes you feel good. My best advice is to let your personality, tastes, and age guide your choice.”

“The most important part of finding a fragrance is, of course, trying it on,” Tanis continues, adding that you can have multiple signature scents, rather than just one, for variety. “Nowadays, it is all about a collection of fragrances. Much like clothing, modern men and women desire a fragrance wardrobe, allowing them the freedom to choose what fragrance to wear daily.”

What is the difference between an eau de toilette and eau de parfum?

Many popular scents fall into these fragrance categories. The difference between the two lies in the concentration of perfume oil each contains, says Tanis. “Eau de toilettes (EDT) have a relatively lighter composition due to their low concentration of perfume oil— between five and 15 percent. On the other hand, Eau de parfums (EDP) have a higher concentration of perfume oils—typically between 12 and 18 percent—meaning they offer a significantly stronger scent that lasts between five and eight hours.”

Choosing between the two might come down to the specific occasion or the weather (i.e., you may lean towards a lighter scent for work and during the summer months, and a richer scent for nights out and during the winter). Check out our perfume terminology guide to learn more about fragrance terms.

Tanis suggests applying fragrance to the pulse points, where the skin is naturally warm, such as the wrists, neck, or inside the elbows. “This will allow the fragrance to warm up and reveal the perfume's sillage (trail) over time.”

#6883. Hublot Just Relaunched Its First Watch. Founder Carlo Crocco Explains How It Changed Everything.

In Milan, celebrating the release of the Classic Fusion Tributo, I got to catch up with the guy who altered the aesthetics of modern watchmaking.

If you spend a lot of time following the world of watches, Hublot’s origin story might feel as familiar as one of the fairytales you heard at bedtime as a child. If you don’t, though, it might feel just as fantastical as some yarn about a princess who feels a pea under a stack of mattresses or a prince who slays a fire-breathing dragon.

You start with an unlikely hero. This time around, it’s an Italian entrepreneur with a big, bold, brazen idea. He wants to make a watch, something unlike anyone has ever seen. It’ll be gold, with an angular case shaped like the porthole on a ship. Not so crazy yet. But wait. Because, breaking entirely with the conventions of the day, the strap won’t be made of exotic leather or precious metal but rubber. Humble, industrial rubber.

This is exactly what Hublot founder Carlo Crocco envisioned nearly half a century ago, and what he brought to the Basel Watch Fair—pretty much the place to be if you wanted to be a part of the business of timekeeping—in 1980. He spent three years developing the idea, all without doing a bit of market research. He just believed in his vision. And then the fair began.

“I was very anxious, because for the first two days, no one came into our booth,” he says. But the anxiety didn’t have long to fester. Word was spreading. And then? People galore. “On the third day, we realized that everyone was speaking about us because it was really something different from all the other watches.”

That first watch—36mm in diameter, with a quartz movement and a black rubber strap—quickly became a hit. Industry buzz gave way to the kind of clientele most brands can only imagine. The King of Greece bought one in Gstaad for himself and another for his brother-in-law, the King of Spain. More royals followed. (“By the way, they’re all related,” Crocco quips.)

But the biggest thing wasn’t getting insiders to a booth at a trade fair or convincing kings to strap Hublot watches—named, if you’re curious, for the French word for porthole—on their wrists. It was changing how the industry thought about the way watches are made.

“At that time, the mentality of watchmaking was very classical,” Crocco says. “They couldn’t think, normal people, of putting together rubber and gold. I personally thought it was a really good idea because it was against the current. It was very risky, but if you don’t risk, you have no result.”

The result was a watch industry that completely recalibrated itself. Crocco says that within four or five years of the original Hublot’s release, the brand was already dealing with imitators. Now, the idea of combining disparate materials is firmly embedded in the minds of both consumers and designers. You can see it on watches from brands that sell at the local department store and brands that only sell to folks who haven’t seen the inside of one of those since the first time their private jet took off. Crocco’s big idea changed the whole game.

Which means it’s a pretty good time to go back to the beginning of this thing. Last month in Milan, Hublot debuted a new take on that first, groundbreaking watch. Dubbed the Hublot Classic Fusion Tributo, it’s an homage but not a nuts-and-bolts recreation. Instead of the original 36mm case, it’s been upsized to a more 2026-appropriate 38mm. And instead of a quartz movement—Crocco thought it was “more practical” at the time but has come around to the enduring appeal of mechanical construction—a self-winding HUB1711 movement with a 50-hour power reserve keeps things ticking along. Oh, and now you can get it in white gold, too.

Then there’s that rubber strap. When Crocco designed the original Hublot, he was insistent that the strap was entirely straight; he felt it was crucial to the aesthetic proposition. On the Tributo, it’s ever-so-slightly, almost imperceptibly tapered, a necessary concession to keep the proportions intact after increasing the case size (which, handily, lets that automatic movement sit comfortably inside).

But, as with the first Hublot, it’s not all about tech specs and watch-nerd particulars. It’s about the spirit of the thing. And the spirit of this watch stays true to that pioneering design. There are twelve screws around the bezel, so you can keep an eye on the hour despite the dial’s lack of indices—something Crocco says was a non-negotiable when he started consulting on the new release. The yellow gold version keeps the abyss-like black lacquer dial and the white gold throws us a tiny (and welcome) curveball by using onyx. The combo of precious metal and rubber still hits, 46 years later.

If you prod him how he feels about the way that he shifted the watch industry, Crocco won’t brag. He left Hublot nearly 20 years ago, when it sold to LVMH. He knows his idea changed things; he’s happy about it. But this isn’t his day job anymore. Still, when he’s asked if he could be considered the godfather of fusing high and low in watchmaking—and you give him a minute to ponder it—he’ll tell you the truth.

#6882. An Expert Guide to Nail Health, According to Professionals

Here’s exactly how to keep them strong and healthy

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

As manicure trends evolve with new lacquers, changing shapes, and viral techniques, beauty lovers often compromise nail health for aesthetics. While many nail formulas have improved over the years to prioritize strength, uniformity, and finish, experts say incremental habits—from lifestyle shifts to resisting the urge to peel off chipped polish—can ultimately play the biggest role in keeping your nails in excellent condition.

“The future of manicures isn’t just about the latest color trends or how long your gel polish lasts, but how well we care for the nails underneath,” Krisztina van der Boom, founder of DryBy, previously told Harper’s Bazaar UK. Hand, foot, and nail expert Margaret Dabbs agrees, urging nail enthusiasts to “Adopt a dedicated nail care routine and stick to it.” But what does an effective routine actually look like? Along with keeping your nails clean and hydrated with an emollient moisturizer, like Caudalíe’s Vinotherapist Hand and Nail Cream, professionals suggest keeping a cuticle oil, such as Dior’s Huile Abricot, in your bag for quick touch-ups.

“Cuticle oil is the one product I would never skip,” says Peach Bling founder Sandra Wei. “Look for a formula with jojoba oil, which has small enough molecules to absorb into the nail and surrounding skin.” Wei encourages her clients to apply it daily, ideally before bed, and to treat it as a small ritual. “A rich hand cream and gloves for dishwashing and cleaning also make a real difference,” continues Wei, “since water and harsh detergents are among the most common causes of dry, weak nails.” Maintaining nail and cuticle hydration is also key for mature nails, as they tend to grow more slowly and hold less moisture as we age, which can contribute to brittleness and vertical ridges.

Soft Services Theraplush Overnight Repair Treatment

Dior Huile Abricot Nutritive Serum for Nails and Cuticles

Professionals have debated whether it’s safe to trim your cuticles, since cuticles guard nail beds against bacteria and infection; that's why they often advise pushing them back instead of removing them entirely. “As long as you're not cutting any live skin and damaging your cuticles, gently pushing them back and buffing away excess skin will actually help improve the health and the appearance of your cuticles and will help make your manicure last as long as possible while looking salon-quality,” says Olivia Van Iderstine, vice president of education and manicure trend expert at Olive & June. Prep your fingertips with cuticle oil or a soak in warm, soapy water before a manicure, then use a disposable orangewood stick or stainless steel pusher, such as JinSoon’s HyperCare Cuticle Pusher + Reducer, to improve polish cohesion and create an elongated nail look.

Consistent, gentle filing can also keep nails strong, regardless of your shape preference, because a smooth surface guards against unwanted splits, rips, and tears that can cause long-term damage. While there’s nothing wrong with a budget-friendly emery board, professionals say investing in the right tools provides the right resistance without compromising your nails' integrity. Dabbs is a fan of crystal nail files because they can help prevent micro-splits that lead to peeling and breakage, though the best results depend on not overfiling.

And if you usually opt for gel polish manicures, they don’t have to be a deal breaker for nail health. Several options, from builder gel to Japanese gel and more, provide longevity, durability, and style without weakening natural nails.

“Japanese gel systems are known for their precision and for their focus on preserving the natural nail,” continues Wei. “The prep is minimal and gentle, so we avoid heavy buffing of the nail plate. The gel itself is applied in fine, structured layers that are flexible and sit close to the natural nail.” Peach Bling prioritizes formulas that are free of HEMA and TPO, two ingredients commonly linked to gel allergies and sensitivities. “The result is a manicure that looks refined, wears beautifully, and treats the nail underneath with care,” explains the founder. Bazaar editors love builder gel, as it works like Japanese gel, supporting natural nails with a rounded apex to reinforce their sturdiness.

Experts advise keeping a gel set on for no longer than four weeks, as regrowth changes the nail's balance, ultimately causing unwanted stress and making breaks more likely. Once gel polish begins to lift or crack, it’s often a sign that removal is necessary—and that process can often be more damaging than simply wearing a gel formula in the first place. “What protects nail integrity is how the gel is applied and, even more importantly, how it is removed,” adds Wei. “With careful, gentle removal, there is no need to take a ‘break’ between appointments.”

For more than 150 years, Harper’s Bazaar has been the preeminent fashion and beauty resource for women at every age. We cover what’s new and what’s next in beauty by working with the world’s leading authorities in dermatology, plastic surgery, cosmetics, skincare, hair care, and fragrance. Every story we publish has been thoroughly researched and vetted by our team of beauty editors and industry experts.

#6881. Aneha Reveals She and Cam Both Planned to Say “No” at Their ‘Love Is Blind’ Wedding; Producers Encouraged Them to Go Anyway

Per Aneha, this was filmed almost *two* years ago.

Aneha and Cam were one of the few couples to actually make it to the altar during Love Is Blind season 11, and ultimately they said “I don’t.” Aneha was technically the one to turn Cam down with a “not yet,” but she just revealed they actually discussed it ahead of time and were both planning to say no. So, why’d they even go through with the wedding? Apparently, production encouraged them to—just in case they changed their minds at the last second.

“Cam and I had talked about it, and we said on both of our parts that most likely, it’s a no, but we should still go to the altar if there’s even the slightest chance that we say yes,” Aneha explained to Vulture. “Chris Coelen, the creator, talked to us about that: He said people, on the day, sometimes go from a no to a yes. And it’s worked out. That’s what we were hopeful for. We said regardless of what the answer was going to be, we’re going to stay together, whether married or engaged. But the day of it was tough because you put everything on, your family’s there, your friends are there, you’re celebrating this beautiful moment.”

Aneha added that “There was a feeling of disappointment mixed in with relief” when she turned Cam down, but that “I have to trust my gut. It didn’t feel right. I felt content, because we had already discussed that no matter what happens, we found each other through this amazing experience for a reason. We talked about being together in a more traditional relationship and seeing how that works. He could actually meet my family. They live in California, so it wasn’t the easiest thing for them to come meet Cam. They are shy and traditional in terms of how to marry.”

Love Is Blind season 11 was filmed almost two years ago, according to Aneha, and she and Cam are both currently single. “I felt like I almost let myself down,” she said. “I wish we had more time, maybe another month. I feel like I would have felt stronger in my opinion either way.”

The pair recently reunited during the reunion, so tune in on Sunday to find out where they stand.

But his campaign for governor was something of a roller coaster.

#6877. I Tried Hailey Bieber’s Favorite Creatine Gummies for Two Months, and I Have Thoughts

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Why these candylike gumdrops have converted me into a creatine lover

The supplement landscape has never been murkier, more confusing, or more overwhelming. While I have a pretty extensive wellness regimen, it feels like there are a million and one powders, pills, and gummies I should be adding to my routine. One supplement that keeps coming into the spotlight is creatine.

I first heard about creatine from my 62-year-old mother, who waxed poetic about the supplement. Then, the influencer of all influencers, Hailey Bieber, posted on Instagram a few weeks later a creatine gummy from Arrae. I thought: That’s it. I’m taking the creatine plunge.

“Creatine works like an energy recycler,” says Roxanne Pero, a Dallas-based obstetrician and gynecologist and part of O Positiv’s Medical Advisory Board. “Every cell in the body needs energy, and that energy comes from a molecule called ATP, or adenosine triphosphate. Creatine helps the body regenerate ATP more quickly, especially when energy runs low, and especially in tissues that use a lot of it, like muscle and the brain.”

As someone who works out five times a week, often in heated classes that push my body to depletion, I figured I was the perfect candidate for creatine. But it’s actually one of those supplements anyone can benefit from; it kicks in at moments when our cells need more energy—not just during strenuous exercise, but also during sleep deprivation and stress, which everyone experiences at some point. It’s even recommended for perimenopause, menopause, and postpartum.

“Active women in their 20s and 30s may benefit from improved training performance, while women approaching menopause or experiencing it may be interested in supporting muscle strength as part of a broader strategy that includes resistance training and adequate protein,” says Rachel Brief, a registered dietitian and wellness advisor at the Lanby.

Recently, the supplement has gained traction among women in their 40s and above. “As a woman enters menopause and postmenopause, the decrease in estrogen contributes to age-related muscle and strength loss,” says Shilpa Raut, the vice president of R&D at Cymbiotika, who holds a master’s degree and a PhD in pharmaceutical sciences. Creatine can help women maintain muscle mass, support steady metabolic health, and improve cognitive resilience, which often declines with age.

If you’re wondering why everyone isn’t already taking this magic supplement, well, I was too. Creatine has long been associated with gym bros, and it has a bad reputation for causing bloating, which is not a risk I’m willing to take as a girl with a sensitive gut. I also wasn’t excited about consuming a chalky white powder. But Arrae’s Tone gummies tout all of the benefits without the bloat, and they come in a candylike form. “Creatine had always been marketed to men, because no one had ever presented it in a way that felt like it was made for women,” Arrae’s cofounder Siffat Haider tells me. “Our customers adopted it immediately, and that demand is why we kept building on the flavors.”

The one caveat with creatine is that you need to drink water to get the full benefits. “Creatine draws water into muscle cells, and that is part of how it works,” says Michael Fredericson, a sports-medicine physiatrist, professor at Stanford University, and Elysium Scientific Advisory Board member. If you’re dehydrated, creatine will pull your body’s existing water supply into your muscles, causing dehydration and hindering normal bodily functions.

I’m now two months into my experiment with Arrae Tone. Whenever I tell people that I started taking creatine, they ask me the same question: “Does it actually work?” My verdict: I don’t plan to stop taking it any time soon. Experts say creatine is especially beneficial as a supplement to strength-training workouts, so I was excited to see how it amplified my workouts. Even a few weeks into taking creatine, I noticed more definition in my abdominals and arms, areas where I’ve historically struggled to see muscle.

The Arrae gummies have no chalky taste and are easy to swallow. I take a lot of different powders and pills, so having a delicious gummy is a nice change of pace. Plus, the dosage is only 3.75 to 5 grams (depending on whether you take three or four gummies), which is on the lower end of the spectrum and has been proven to be effective without causing the dreaded bloating. And it’s creatine monohydrate, which is the gold standard of creatine, Brief explains. Still, they’re not my only form of creatine. On days when I’d rather add a powder to a smoothie or my water to boost hydration, I love the Elysium Health powders; they’re only 5 grams, so they’re easy on my stomach, and they come in delicious fruity flavors that taste like nostalgic lemonade.

#6873. After I Kicked Drugs and Alcohol, I Found a New Addiction: Cheating on My Fiancée

Thirteen years of sobriety taught me that the most dangerous thing for an addict isn’t getting caught—it’s getting away with it. And I keep getting away with it.

Ethical nonmonogamy relies on a foundation of total transparency. Partners openly agree to terms and continually negotiate them together. Betrayals such as infidelity can take many shapes and break the agreement a couple has made, monogamous or not. For people in recovery, however, the adrenaline of secret keeping can feel familiar. Clinicians call this behavioral, or process, addiction—in which compulsion lies in the thrill of the chase rather than the payoff. It doesn’t excuse betrayal, but it can help explain how a person committed to honesty ends up living a lie.

For this installment of the Secret Lives of Men, we spoke to Owen,* a 36-year-old brand strategist with 13 years of sobriety who spent most of the first two years of his relationship cheating on the partner he plans to marry. His story begs the question: What does it mean to carry out “living amends,” an Alcoholics Anonymous term for permanently changing your behavior for the better, when you aren’t ready to confess?

*Names and identifying details have been changed to protect anonymity.

Once a week, my fiancée has a therapy session over a video call that she takes from our bedroom. We both work from home, so while that happens, I’m usually barricaded in my office with noise-canceling headphones on, keeping up with work. Recently, I came out to the kitchen to pour a coffee after her hour was up, and she wasn’t home.

She takes walks, so there was no reason to panic. But my mind went where it has gone a lot lately: She found out. By whatever cruel trick of fate, she knew, and she was out there walking it off, letting the anger curdle. When she came back, she would bring hell with her.

I called once. She didn’t pick up. I didn’t try again. For half an hour, I paced the apartment unable to get a single thing done. I checked my phone every few seconds. I rehearsed what I’d say when she walked through the door: which parts I’d confess, which parts I’d still try to save. I worried myself sick. When she finally walked back in, it turned out she’d just been on a long phone call with her sister. She took off her headset, kissed me hello, and asked what I had in mind for dinner. My heart kept pounding long after she’d walked into the kitchen.

Until two years ago, I could say with real pride that I had never cheated on anyone. Not once. Not even when I was drinking and doing all those drugs, back when I lied about nearly everything else. I’d had opportunities, but infidelity was the one line I never crossed.

Advanced 12-step work taught me a strange paradox: Sometimes you have to sit with the agonizing truth that you are doing something wrong. That you aren’t ready or able to stop. I’ve built 13 years of sobriety on the idea that we are only as sick as our secrets. But I should admit something up front: I’m good at telling stories, especially about myself. Even now, I catch myself deciding which parts to share. So here it is: For the better part of two years, I cheated on the woman I’m going to marry. She still doesn’t know.

In recovery, you learn that the most dangerous thing that can happen to a person with an addiction isn’t getting caught—it’s getting away with it. And every time you skate by, the lie gets a little easier to tell. The disease gets a little more room to grow. I first learned that at 23. I had to learn it again in my 30s.

I come from a long line of talented, tortured people. My father sold commercial real estate and spent years in and out of a quiet, high-functioning addiction to painkillers. He went away for treatment twice. My mother was raised by an alcoholic and carried a lot of anger. My parents divorced when I was young, both remarried badly, and neither of their houses was a safe place emotionally.

At 19, while in college, I fell into a relationship I can only describe as wickedly codependent. We went through two miscarriages together and became trauma bonded. I didn’t know how to grieve, so I was drinking heavily and taking almost anything I could find: stimulants, psychedelics, dissociatives. Just before I turned 21, I caught my girlfriend in a lie so profound it ripped the wool off my eyes: She had been sleeping with my best friend for nearly the entire time we’d been together. One of the miscarried babies was actually his. I ended things the day I found out and took the situation as the perfect excuse to keep killing myself slowly.

I didn’t make it a year out of college before the consequences caught up with me. The fall after graduation, I was driving home from a concert when I got pulled over for a dead taillight. I was relatively sober and polite and was sure I’d talked my way out of it—right up until the officer asked me to empty my pockets. Inside were a bag of powder that I’d been promised was Molly and a few pills I didn’t have a prescription for. They booked me and sent everything off to the state lab. For three months, nothing happened. Meanwhile, I painted myself further into the corner before the floor ultimately fell out. When the charge finally came, I began a year and a half of court dates and probation.

As a first offender, I pled into a court-mandated, recovery-focused diversion program with regular drug tests. I followed the program to a T, at least for a while.

One weekend, when house-sitting alone for a neighbor, I took a drink out of boredom and pure spite. No test caught it. Then I lied about it to my counselors, the court, and my family. A month later, guilt finally won out, and I admitted it to my sponsor. A few days later, he brought it back up and told me I had to come clean to everyone.

“The fact that you were willing to conceal that from everyone for this long,” he said, “is the only evidence you need of how sick you are.”

The problem wasn’t the drink. It was how easily I hid it. I surrendered to the program and handed my life over to a higher power. I haven’t had a drink since.

I went celibate by choice for three years. I did a lot of therapy and learned that I had swung from codependent to avoidant. I had finally regained control, and I didn’t want to hand myself to anyone. Then, in my late 20s, I decided to give dating another shot.

Before long, I leaned into ethical nonmonogamy and dated as “solo poly,” which means I was seeing multiple people without a primary partner. Casual dating became my primary hobby. Some connections were purely physical; some were friends with benefits; some went emotionally deep. At my peak, I was sleeping with six people, two or three times a week. I felt I was winning at something. I was always honest with every partner about who I was, sobriety included. But I wasn’t honest with myself about why I needed so much of it.

Then, later in therapy, I came to understand that the dopamine that keeps people in an addiction loop is driven less by the thing itself than by the anticipation of getting it. Dating apps are built on that anticipation. For me, there were three hits: the match, the first nudes, the first hookup. In the program, we say the disease of addiction wears as many faces as it needs to. The chase was one of those faces.

I matched with Maya as she was getting ready to leave on a six-week work trip to Thailand. We messaged far longer than usual, trading long voice notes across the 11-hour time difference—her from hotel lobbies at night, me on morning walks. By the time we finally met, I already knew how she laughed. Our first date was electric, the best of my life. We sat at the bar until they started stacking chairs, talking about family, grief, books that had saved us. She was self-sufficient and emotionally deep. She was great at conversation. She was beautiful.

A few months into our relationship, we curled up on her couch on a Sunday afternoon and talked about ethical nonmonogamy. We’d both dabbled in it, but neither of us had done it inside a primary relationship. I told her I wanted it. She was open to it. We agreed to start monogamous and build the foundation first, but we never said for how long.

The truth is, our sex wasn’t great. Early on, I suggested something new in bed, and she went quiet and turned toward the wall. Neither of us brought it up the next morning. I internalized the idea that I couldn’t ask for what I wanted, that this was just how it would be until we opened things up. Instead of communicating, I let the resentment build.

Then, one afternoon at a coffee shop across town, I ran into Bree, a woman I used to hook up with. She had been the perfect friend with benefits: incredible chemistry but no desire for a relationship. We stayed and talked for an hour. When she mentioned she lived ten minutes away, neither of us pretended not to know what that meant. By that evening, I was at her place. It was the first time in my life I had been unfaithful in any way, and I felt awful. When I went home, I waited for the consequences. They never came.

A big part of addiction is justification. We were planning to be nonmonogamous, I told myself, so the way I’d just conducted myself was basically equivalent. It wasn’t that bad. I can see now how thin that logic was. Nonmonogamy runs on transparency and consent. I was keeping a secret.

Over the next year and a half, my infidelities came and went. A few more times with Bree. A handful of times with anonymous women in other cities. Trans women, sex workers. I traveled for work, and I took advantage of the distance. I kept my phone face down, hid dating apps, used end-to-end-encrypted texting. For each time I followed through, there were five times I stopped myself. Each close call made me feel like I had it under control.

Strangely, the woman I cheated with most also kept me from making worse mistakes. Bree became my confidante, the only person I was fully honest with about any of it. One night, I arranged to meet a sex worker. I was sitting in my car outside the hotel when clarity hit. The only thing I could think to do was call Bree. She talked me down. She and I ended up having sex that night. The program has a phrase for that kind of thinking: I want what I want when I want it.

Meanwhile, the relationship I was betraying kept getting better. We slowly began to practice nonmonogamy, and it took us far longer than either of us expected. We listened to the audiobook version of Polysecure by Jessica Fern together. We’d pause so often to talk that a half-hour chapter took two hours. It gave me a way to ask for what I wanted without it feeling like an attack on her. One night, Maya paused the book and asked me, plainly, how she could do better. For the first time, I believed she meant it, and I told her. The sex got better. Then, much better.

The final close call came in June. Bored at my desk, I connected with someone on an anonymous hookup site, and we moved to texting. A few messages in, after I’d sent a couple of nudes, my phone lit up with a long blackmail threat. Attached was a screenshot of Maya’s Instagram. This kind of thing had happened four or five times before, back when I was single. Go ahead, I would say. I never considered paying, and no one ever followed through. But this time, I closed my laptop, walked to the bathroom, sat on the edge of the tub until my hands stopped shaking, and blocked the number. This time, I had something to lose. I was forced to face how stupid I was being.

I shared the story in therapy. My therapist, who usually lets me do most of the talking, leaned back, looked at his clipboard, and said, “What the fuck are you doing? This is like the 20th time something like this has happened.”

I didn’t have an answer. Nothing had ever happened to me. That was the whole point.

This past August, on a weekend trip to the Catskills with friends, I proposed to Maya. She said yes before I could finish the question. I meant every word I said that weekend.

Maya and I have now officially opened our relationship. We’ve mostly played as a couple. When she sees someone on her own, she tells me everything the next morning. Last month, I sexted with a woman for days and chose not to tell her. Though I have permission, I can’t seem to give up the secrecy.

Growing up in the houses I did, I hid the truth to keep the peace. Recovery taught me to say it anyway, but I’ve only ever practiced with people who can’t leave, like a sponsor or a therapist. Telling Maya is different. Sometimes, I reverse the question: What if she were the one carrying a secret like this? I like to think that I’d find a way to accept it, because through everything I’ve done, I have never loved her any less.

In the program, when you can’t make amends without causing further harm, you make what’s called living amends. It starts with stopping the behavior. Then you try to love better, act cleaner, and stay present day after day, without asking for absolution. That’s where I am now. I pray regularly that this pain is my consequence. If that’s my penance, I’ll carry it.

16 New Products Esquire’s Editors Loved This Week

#6872. Zillennials Don’t Feel the Need to Grow Up, and Club Kid Knows Why

Jordan Firstman’s deferred coming-of-age tale speaks to the realities of living in an unwieldy time

I’m 29, an age that has become a catalyst for conversations about what it means to be an adult, settling down (or not), and—perhaps above all—how to show up for my own well-being. (Do I need to party less?) It’s a volley of topics that are often the subject of yearly think pieces, even when we know the conclusions they’ll reach. Millennials and Zillennials feel pressured by older generations to live a conventional and ambitious life, even as they’ve squeezed young people’s hope for a future.

The New York Times detailed this dilemma just last month, speaking to 30-somethings across New York City who felt discouraged by their situation and, to put it plainly, left behind. The article pointed out that the long-running Harvard Youth Poll found that for the first time ever, most young Americans don’t believe they will surpass their parents financially. In the last 30 years, the cost of living has skyrocketed, and, today, mounting loans and debt among young people have made a house feel out of reach. Looking even younger, Gen Z is feeling the brunt of the economy, with Fortune finding that two thirds of parents say their Gen Z adult kids rely on them (and Playboy going so far as to connect the housing crisis to the sex decline). Why stop partying if the rent’s too high, if there’s no reasonable expectation that you’ll find security, buy a house, or live easily past 2050?

This growing frustration is likely why audiences—including myself—are connecting so deeply with influencer-turned-director Jordan Firstman’s sensational feature debut, Club Kid. The film offers a tender look at arrested development and unexpected fatherhood, but, above all, it delivers an incisive look at the struggle to let go of youth when the world feels increasingly grim. The idea of partying instead of worrying is alluring: to exist in a space free from responsibility. Peter (Firstman), a 30-something party promoter deeply enmeshed in New York’s underground queer club scene, lives in the apartment his late mother left him and sells drugs on the side, and his only real daily concern is recovering for the next party. The moment he crosses the threshold of any club, he plunges into a fugue state fueled by drugs and sex. Peter clings to his youth (his name aptly echoing Peter Pan syndrome), shirks all responsibilities, and actively defers a much-needed inward look.

Peter’s wake-up call arrives in the form of a 10-year-old boy named Arlo (Reggie Absolom)—a son he unknowingly fathered years after hooking up with a man who insisted they turn their dalliance into a threesome with a woman. A decade after that fateful night, the woman’s best friend (played with drug-fueled frenzy by Kirby Howell-Baptiste) shows up at his door, a young child in tow, with news that the mother has died by suicide and Peter needs to step up. It’s a parable tailor-made for a worn-down generation that sees no reason to stop the party. It takes a ketamine stork of sorts to change course.

Club Kid revels in the growing pains. Firstman avoids reducing the conflict to a simple clash over parenting Arlo or his personal struggle to break free from the party lifestyle; instead, Club Kid unfolds as a tender parallel coming-of-age story, where Peter grows up as he learns to parent Arlo, a quiet, curious kid grappling with the loss of his mother. Firstman embraces the missteps, letting us see the beauty of his life as it intermingles with his newfound parenting. The whirlwind of responsibility and unwitting fatherhood plays like a fairy tale—a wondrous reality that feels increasingly distant from what a 30-something can reasonably expect today.

Watching Club Kid, I considered my own parents, who conceived me (by happy accident, I’m told) at 27 and 29. They worked at a pizza restaurant before opening their own bar. My childhood felt wrapped up in Atlanta’s nightlife scene; somehow, completely inexperienced, they still learned to balance their lifestyle with their newfound roles as parents. I lived as the only kid (with my two-years-younger brother) in my parents’ world for several years, often sitting with the bar regulars for hours while my parents worked or being actively watched over by the cooks. It felt like tapping into another world, but it also brought us closer. They didn’t give up their life for me; they made me a part of it. These days, this story sounds like a romantic ideal, a whirlwind adventure only possible without looming loans and the high cost of living. I simply couldn’t imagine doing the same today.

What makes Peter’s story compelling is how he navigates the responsibilities of fatherhood without fully forgetting himself. Some critics have challenged the film, claiming that it’s stigmatizing the queer party scene, but in his telling, he doesn’t rebuke his former lifestyle but rather the parts of it that were bad for him. His friend even welcomes the news of his fatherhood with excitement, spending time with Arlo without passing judgment. Even if he eventually needs to distance himself from these friends’ party habits, it doesn’t need a blowout fight. Life happens, and people move on.

That’s why it’s important that Firstman returns to the real problem at the end of the movie. A vindictive call to social services by Peter’s ex-best friend (Cara Delevigne) leads to Arlo’s deportation and the new father-son duo’s separation. It brings to light the real-life anxieties looming over this heartwarming story. Sure, Peter did not file the proper adoption papers, and his lack of money becomes damningly apparent when he stares down health care premiums and legal bills in the face of an unrelenting bureaucracy.

Firstman refuses to provide a pretty picture. He instead studies the anxieties of young millennials, a generation perennially unsure of the world around them, and distills them into a heartwarming, deferred coming-of-age story. Firstman dreams of who we can become, if only given the chance to grow up in a world where steady ground is hard to find. Peter is growing up alongside his son, hiccups and all.

#6871. The Wicker Husband Is Attentive, Devoted, and Made of Branches. The Bar Is...Interesting

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

“Monster smut” is a booming category, “Creature Cocks” are their own category of sex toys, and right now Wicker has a 94 percent score on Rotten Tomatoes. And while I know people have always been into scales, wings, tentacles, and more, the actual material and texture aspect of wicker really has me stumped (pun definitely intended).

I made it a point to watch Wicker as soon as it came out. The trailer looked fun and exciting and unlike anything I’ve seen before. And at the end of the day, I love a good romance—even when it is between woman and basket. But I couldn’t help but wonder if there was something deeper going on with the buzz around the movie. A referendum on modern romance, perhaps? But where do the branches fit in?

“I think it says something quite revealing that being nice, considerate, and sexually attentive can be presented as the ultimate package,” Anna Richards, sex educator and founder of Frolicme.com—an ethical erotica site—tells Cosmopolitan. “These shouldn't be extraordinary qualities; they should be the foundations of a healthy relationship.”

She explains that women want to feel desired, respected, and understood, without having to sacrifice their own in the process of attaining these things. And the husband in Wicker, played by Alexander Skarsgaard, is all of these things. He is attentive, he fixes things around the house (without being asked, most importantly), and he is a vigorous lover.

“The fantasy might be an exaggerated fictional man, but the underlying desires are very real,” Richard says.

But in the current state of heterosexual dating, the growing sentiment for a while now is that women are not getting these basic, bare-minimum attributes out of the men they date.

And the attitudes aren't improving with younger generations: A 2026 global study by Ipsos and King's College London found that 31 percent of Gen-Z men believe a wife should always obey her husband, while 33 percent think husbands should have the final say on important decisions. Meanwhile, according to Pew Research Center, 66 percent of women under 50 who have used dating apps have experienced at least one form of unwanted behavior on the platforms, including unsolicited sexual messages, continued contact after rejecting someone, name-calling, or threats. So, between the manosphere's growing influence, the normalization of misogynistic attitudes, and the very real unpleasantness of dating straight men, it's perhaps not so surprising that women are finding romance in increasingly unconventional places.

Couples therapist Doriel Jacov, JD, LCSW explains that the socialization of men today plays a role. From childhood, he says, boys are taught to be strong and self-sufficient, without being given much insight into meeting someone’s emotional needs. Women, on the other hand, are expected to do it all: be present, attuned to others’ emotions, and handle everything on their own.

“Anger and overworking have historically been more socially accepted ways for men to express emotion than vulnerability,” Jacov says. “As a result, many women may find their emotional needs unmet, particularly as it relates to vulnerability and emotional presence.”

So when you see the Skarsgaard take the time to learn from the Fisherwoman (Olivia Coleman), act on her needs, and prioritize her happiness, the fact that he’s made of branches really feels like an irrelevant, secondary detail.

But, it really does need to be acknowledged that a made-to-order husband isn’t the healthiest thing, and not only because wicker isn’t super safe for your vagina. (I mean look what’s happening to the people who are in relationships with basically that, aka chat bots.) Deep down, we know that a great partnership includes two imperfect people working together to support one another through respect and love. But we can all learn from the Wicker Husband, regardless.

“The Wicker Husband is gentle, attentive and devoted, and his tenderness unsettles the whole village,” therapist Silvia Dutchevici, LCSW says. This is because even the men of the village think that someone who is a good partner is a rare sighting!

“That may be what so many of us are longing for: not a man under a woman’s control, but a man who is free to be kind, present, and vulnerable.”
