#6811. The Apple Watch Deals You Need to Look for This Black Friday

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

We’re expecting record-low deals in the coming weeks—but for now, we’ll take $250 off.

It’s no secret that Apple has been dominating tech headlines this year. But between the news of its recent price hikes on MacBooks and iPads and the release of the iPhone Duo (the tech giant’s first foldable), not much fanfare has been made about the new generations of the Apple Watch. As far as we’re concerned, that’s a damn shame, because they’re well worth the upgrade—especially if you can find one on sale like we just did.

Apple Watches usually go on deepest discount during Amazon sales, Black Friday included. And while only two models are marked down right now (the Ultra 4 and Ultra 3), we know a thing or two about how Apple deals work. In the coming weeks, prices will drop and these wearables will sell out like hotcakes, which is why you’ll want to plan what you’re going to buy now.

Below, we’re breaking down the differences between each model—and peeling back the curtain on our price-tracking prowess—so you’re all set come Black Friday. If you don’t take the plunge on one before then, that is.

Commerce editor Luke Guillory argued that compared to other fitness trackers on the market, Apple was lagging behind with its lack of readiness score functionality—until now. The Apple Watch Series 12 marks the debut of the brand’s very own readiness score thanks to a revamped tracking system. Plus, the watch now measures your heart rate more frequently to deliver Heart Rate Variability (HRV) readings every five minutes. If you’re trying to pay closer attention to your health this year, it’s a smart buy, made all the sweeter when it inevitably goes back on sale for Black Friday.

For October Prime Day, this brand-new watch surprisingly dropped to just $350, so we’re expecting a similar—if not deeper—discount in the coming weeks.

While the Apple Watch Ultra 4 isn’t necessarily reinventing the wheel, it did get the same advanced health tracking and readiness score features from the Apple Watch Series 12, so if you’re looking to get your first Ultra model, it’s worth grabbing—especially when it’s $100 off. Not to mention, Guillory is partial to the new ocean-inspired, semi-translucent rubber watch bands.

That said, if you’re not someone who needs the shiny new gadget immediately or who won’t take advantage of these souped-up health features—you’re not going to miss out on much if you score an older model on sale, like this $250 discount on the Apple Watch Ultra 3. Associate Commerce Editor Bryn Gelbart went so far as to dub it the “best Apple Watch ever” when it was released last fall.

“I was never tempted to upgrade to the premium option, but the Apple Watch Ultra 3 changed my mind,” Gelbart said. “It turns out I didn't quite know what I was missing.” It has a bright 49mm display, satellite messaging features, a durable titanium case, and a long lasting battery (an impressive 42 hours under normal use and 72 hours on Low Power Mode). Gelbart recommends it for cyclists, runners, or the adventure-prone.

At 28% off, this is the best pre-Black Friday deal to shop as of publication, although this model has dipped as low as $600 before.

If you’re looking for Apple’s most affordable watch, we suggest turning your attention to the entry-level Apple Watch SE 3. It was actually the watch that converted Gelbart to Apple wearables in the first place, arguing that its beauty is in its simplicity.

“It's an accurate and predictive workout tracker. It counts my steps and activity and connects to my phone to play music and receive messages and calls,” he says. “If you’re new to the Apple Watch and want to dip your toes, this is where I recommend you start.” It has the same sleep tracking features, like nightly sleep score and sleep apnea notifications, but lacks the ECG app or hypertension notifications of its newer siblings. It also has an 18-hour battery life so you’ll need to charge most nights.

This dropped to a record-low $199 during October Prime Day, so we’re hopeful that a similar price cut will emerge for Black Friday, but this is by far the most affordable Apple Watch on the market, even at full price.

Our philosophy when it comes to shopping for Apple tech during Black Friday? If you find the watch with the features you need at a price you can stomach, it’s worth grabbing quickly. During Prime Day, a handful of watches sold out completely—and you won’t want your wearable of choice to meet the same fate before you check out.

#6810. 7 Best Ways to Care for Gray Hair in the Colder Months, From Boosting Hydration to Restoring Shine

Easy tricks to keep silver strands healthy and hydrated when the temperature drops.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

If red carpets and runways are anything to go by this autumn, silver strands are the trend du jour. Emma Thompson, Olivia Colman, and Sarah Jessica Parker are leading the former camp, each having debuted an elegant gray transformation in recent weeks, while shows such as Lanvin, Akrin, and Andreas Kronthaler for Vivienne Westwood spotlighted gray hair at Paris Fashion Week.

It seems, then, that there’s no better time to embrace gray and silver hair. But for anyone seeking to follow suit, it’s important to note that cooler temperatures can affect the typical upkeep involved. “Cold weather dries out gray hair, making it feel rough, brittle, and more prone to breakage and frizz,” says John Clark, senior colorist at Nicola Clarke salon. “This is because the cold, low-humidity air pulls moisture from the hair, while indoor heating further dries it out.”

Since gray hair indicates a structural change in the hair (as it’s caused by a loss of melanin in the strands), it’s often more prone to dryness, regardless of the weather. Dr. Aamna Adel, founder of Rhute Haircare, explains: “Gray or white hair often has fewer natural lipids and a rougher cuticle, which means it can feel drier and coarser. It also reflects light differently, so it can look dull or wiry.”

She adds that both the texture of the hair and the scalp can change when temperatures drop; flakiness and irritation can be common consequences.

Thankfully, there is a range of easy ways to restore both back into top form. With winter firmly on the horizon, discover the seven swaps and changes you can make to your gray hair routine to best care for it in the weeks ahead.

A healthy scalp will ensure that gray strands are stronger and denser, according to hairstylist and founder of Jo Nova, Joseph Yildiz.

Adds Dr. Adel: “Look for ceramides, fatty acids, shea butter, and panthenol to rebuild the lipid barrier; niacinamide to support scalp health; and antioxidants like vitamin E, stem cell extracts, and green tea extract to protect against oxidative stress, which accelerates graying and dryness. Amino acids and peptides can also help strengthen the hair shaft over time.”

As gray hair naturally feels coarser, the last thing you want is to strip it with shampoos that contain harsh sulfates. Opt for hydrating formulas or cleansing conditioners, and while the hair is damp, apply a leave-in conditioner.

Incorporate a silver shampoo and conditioner once a week. “It dulls any yellow or brassy tones and helps reflect the light, making hair look shiny and glossy,” says Clark.

Alongside this, a clarifying formula used once every two weeks is useful, too, especially if you tend to use a lot of styling products. “It will lightly cleanse any product buildup or mineral deposits in the hair. Sometimes white hair can appear yellow, and this solves that,” says Jo Cockle at John Frieda Salon. Always follow with a bond-building and moisturizing hair mask to keep it strong and soft.

Philip Kingsley Pure Blonde/Silver Shampoo

K18 Full-Size Leave-In Molecular Repair Hair Mask

Avoid stepping outside into the cold right after air-drying your hair. “This can be incredibly damaging to hair that is already prone to dryness. The water in your hair can expand in freezing temperatures, which can lift the hair’s cuticle and lead to split ends,” says Hollie Rose Clarke, hairstylist and Living Proof ambassador.

Instead, she recommends using a hydrating product to help keep hair soft and conditioned while air-drying. Clarke is a fan of Living Proof Air-Dry Styler, which contains hemisqualane for moisture that won’t weigh down fine strands. You can also experiment with a nourishing hair oil such as Moroccanoil’s Treatment, which is brimming with omega-6 for deep hydration.

In the evenings, Dr. Adel recommends wearing a silk or satin-lined scarf or bonnet to prevent friction and static as you sleep.

Replenish lost moisture with cocooning hair masks and treatments. Clark recommends incorporating keratin-based formulas to restore strength, while Clarke name-checks amino acids to increase hair’s hydrophobicity (this ultimately helps to lock in moisture), and hemisqualane to seal the cuticles so locks appear silkier.

Sam McKnight Hair Rich Nourish Conditioner

Ideally, try not to overstyle your hair with heated tools. “This can leave hair broken, dry, and even discolored,” says Clark. But, if you must use them (and most of us do prefer to), shield your strands with a heat protector, even if you’re just running a hair dryer through it. Look for one with hydrating, frizz-managing qualities to ease flyaways.

Wella Ultimate Smooth Miracle Hair Oil Serum

There’s a reason why some may find their grays looking lackluster. “Gray hair is more porous, meaning there is less natural protection from environmental factors (such as urban pollution) than with pigmented hair. This can be what causes a lack of shine or discoloration,” Clarke explains.

Modern shield formulas deliver hydration and protection against both heat tools and environmental damage.

Oribe Invisible Defense Universal Protection Spray

Beauty Works Solaré UV Leave-In Conditioning Mist

As a finishing touch, a small amount of hair oil will add extra shine to your grays, but a glossing treatment delivers luster that lasts. At-home glosses (which are often applied pre-conditioner or in place of it, left for a few minutes, then washed out) promise instant results. You can also use a semipermanent conditioning tint such as Shrine Drop It to revive the color of your grays.

#6809. These 9 Brands Are Making the Best Dark-Wash Jeans on the Market

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Moody, rich, and sophisticated—everything you want to project with your dressing.

In the world of denim, think of dark wash jeans like a good Scotch. It exists within a broader umbrella, but it’s a little more weathered and more mature. In the grand scheme of jeans, you really can’t go wrong when it comes to different washes. For clarity, we use dark wash to describe lightly washed dark jeans, raw sanforized jeans, and the real nerdy loomstate shrink-to-fit stuff. Why? Because they are all, visually, dark wash. For us, it’s exactly how you should buy your jeans.

Jeans inherently skew casual. But the subdued color of dark wash allows it to pass for something a little more refined. Where other denim gets processed and faded after being dyed, dark wash hasn’t—letting it keep that subdued inky hue that veers slightly toward “polished trouser” territory. They’re more rugged than a pair of slacks, but just formal and sophisticated enough that you can probably wear them around the office as a sort of nonchalantly edgy work pant.

You won’t need to hunt far or hard for quality and stylish dark washes. Most, if not all, of the Esquire-editor-approved jeans and denim brands also offer darker colors—making it as easy as buying your favorite sweater in another color. Find your favorite below.

Rag & Bone knows what it’s good at and sticks to it. (It’s denim...they’re good at denim.) Our reviewers especially like how the brand approaches its sizing and fits. It’s a super simple and straightforward number system that starts at 1 and goes up to 4. Fit 1 offers a slimmer silhouette, while Fit 4 gives that classic straight look.

For their dark wash—which Rag & Bone calls “Solomon”—the brand uses its very own rb Selvedge, which it developed in partnership with one of Japan's premier denim mills. This will help the jeans naturally develop a nice patina over time to really show just how much you love your denim.

If you ask Esquire shopping editor Luke Guillory what jeans to buy, he’s going to tell you these. He’s already waxed poetic about Wrangler’s original Cowboy Cut in our denim buying guide, so we’ll just cut to the chase: they’re super flattering and at an ultra-competitive price (though he advises bigger guys should opt for the more relaxed cut).

If you’re looking for something under $100, Gap’s more than likely where it’s at. The retailer has spent a ton of time and effort in reupping its basics—jeans especially. The brand’s classic straight silhouette is, at this point in time, better than the offering from the big-name brand that starts with “L” and rhymes with WiFi.

People hunt for vintage Levi’s for a reason. These 505s are a reproduction of the brand’s iconic style for 1967. As Luke describes in our denim buying guide, the fit will “hug up top, drape down low.” “They're universally flattering and classic cut, so you don't have to worry about them going out of style,” he says. “As long as they're holding on, you'll look good.”

We’ve already rounded up a few Todd Snyder pieces we’d recommend buying, and these dark indigo worn wash jeans are one more thing to add to the list. Available in three different fits—slim, straight, and relaxed—it’s made from an all-cotton denim that balances comfort and durability.

This denim from Ralph Lauren’s Americana sublabel is an absolute classic. It’s got a distinctly Western feel with its deep 13.75 oz selvedge denim, while still offering that sort of rugged sophistication you want from a dark wash jean.

Little known fact, but Acne Studios actually started out with jeans. Over 20 years later, it’s still their bread and butter—with funky pieces that embody a “rock and roll edge,” as Luke describes them. This groovy wide-legged pair especially gives off a sort of “Lenny-Kravitz-meets-Steven-Tyler” air of vintage swagger.

We’re long-time fans of Buck Mason. As Esquire contributor Matthew Medendrop describes, the brand has been “quietly doing some of the most exciting things in contemporary menswear” for the last few years—and their denim is no exception. The brand’s “full saddle” jeans take inspiration from the classic vintage blue jeans, think Levi’s 501s circa like the ‘60s. Pair that with the deep dark wash, and you got one sexy pair of jeans.

3Sixteen is one of the premier brands for Esquire’s denim nerds. It gets into the nitty-gritty stuff we care about, but since it’s based here in North America—and not in Japan—it’s a lot easier to get your hands on. To break down the naming, RS means relaxed straight, what we think is the easiest to wear from the brand. The 100xk tag is the type of denim—custom woven for the brand by Kabuki Mills. It’s rough at first, be warned, but it softens and fades like no other.

#6805. From Caramelized Amber to Bombshell Blowouts, These Are Fall’s Biggest Hair Trends

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Hairstylists share the buzziest fall hair trends their clients can’t get enough of.

When dried leaves in varying hues of orange, red, and yellow crunch under our feet, it’s basically a bat signal that the best season is underway. Fall sits right in the middle of summer’s balmy rays and winter’s biting chill, with a refreshing briskness that won’t necessarily expedite reversion for curly hair going straight and won’t dry out freshly bleached and/or dyed strands. The options are endless in this kind of weather, which can make decision-making feel a bit overwhelming. Do you try a new color? A bob cut? Maybe a pixie? According to the hottest hair trends of the season, you can do it all.

The transition from summer to fall is an important one for your hair. “Hair can be a little more compromised or porous based on the environment, from swimming pools and sun in the summer. Going from fall to winter, hair treatments to encourage hydration and strength are the easiest way to get your hair back on track,” says Eric Marshall Wright, stylist and owner of Eric Marshall Salon in Nashville, Tennessee. With a proper hair routine at home, your hair becomes strong enough to withstand your next hair transformation—that’s where fall’s biggest hair trends come in.

If you’re itching for a new hairdo, we polled several hairstylists, including Wright, to see what they predict will be the most head-turning hairstyles of the season, straight from their very own salons. Whether you’re a balayage connoisseur or tempted to do a bob, allow this list of trends to inspire you ahead of your next appointment.

Here’s a subtle take on the popular balayage technique that works with your hair color, creating a soft gradient with lowlights and highlights. “These highlights are soft, tone-on-tone highlights with dimension—think of them as a soft whisper in an overstimulated world. They look underdone instead of overdone; it’s a delicate hair color that’s sustainable and grounded. The look is effortlessly cool, glossy, and glazed for a finish that’s organically imperfect,” says Leslie Shores, color specialist at Eric Marshall Salon. This shade is perfect for “anyone who prefers a timeless, sustainable color. It’s achieved with no bleach, lightener, balayage, or ammonia-free demi-permanent hair color. Ask your colorist for soft tone-on-tone color.”

Sometimes the road to blonde is far too intimidating from dark brown or black. Shores’ answer to that gives you a lift without veering too much into blonde territory. “Cocoa brondette,” she says, is “a rich brunette with soft ribbons of deep indulgent brown shades.” The color is reminiscent of a rich cup of hot cocoa, with swirls of light brown throughout. “This shade looks best on someone with an olive skin tone, and can be achieved with demi-permanent or permanent hair color or with balayage. Ask your colorist for an all-over color with ammonia-free color around a level seven, six, or five; they can also paint soft ribbons of balayage.”

While most of the world is obsessed with bobs and pixies, another percentage of the population still favors long locks. Dimitris Giannetos, celebrity hairstylist and Great Lengths ambassador, says long hair isn’t going anywhere. “Hair can take a long minute to grow, so I have the help of Great Lengths, especially the Linea Wefts, to achieve the perfect, lived-in long hair,” he shares. His favorite hair trend this season? “CEO blowout,” which looks polished “but secure enough to get a bit messy.” This looks even better when paired with a “quiet, lived-in color, with more wispy, secret highlights versus an obvious balayage,” he adds. Your tools to achieve this are simple: “I use three different shades of the Great Lengths Line Wefts. They have such a fine spine, which makes them easy to blend and fully match your client’s color with an extra twist. They are super flat on the scalp, so they blend together perfectly without unwanted volume on the sides. I always recommend razoring the wefts at the end to create the perfect blend,” Giannetos says.

Wright concurs, adding that layers can elevate long styles that “looks effortless and moves.” Placement matters when it comes to layering hair, depending on the style you wear most often. “Layers can be as short as collarbone so you can easily put it up, but this is more about wearing it down and long-styled. Think hippie boho or 60s and long like Pocahontas. The cut is accomplished by point cutting and dry cutting techniques to make it soft and lived-in, not overly aggressive but effortless layers,” Wright shares.

If you want a louder balayage, Shores encourages you to opt for “rich shades of buttery gold and copper throughout the hair that look warm, rich, cozy, and earthy.” The gradient of hues might immediately scream summer, but it offers a toasted finish that “adds exotic warmth, depth, and dimension to the hair that feels like golden hour. Ask your colorist to paint big balayage pieces mainly in the front of the hair to match the volume and layers. These can be foiled or painted on. It looks best on brunettes and someone with thicker hair with layers.”

Already blonde? Shores suggests “bright, airy white-blonde balayage highlights that are airy and dreamy placed perfectly, as they carry a lightness to them that make them look ethereal and serene.” This look works great for those who are already “bright platinum without the constant touch up. [It’s also] perfect also for grey blending. This look can be achieved by using bleach balayage or foilyage. Ask your colorist to use bleach balayage or foilyage to get as light as possible!” she adds.

“Thanks to Off Campus and [actress] Ella Bright, we’re gravitating towards this shoulder-length bob that is worn flipped, sleek, curled, or blown out,” Wright says. Pictured here, this bob is marked by an asymmetrical blunt cut and soft waves throughout. “The cut needs to have facial shape and neck width taken into consideration, as those will cause different results, as well as the natural density and hair diameter,” Wright adds.

With summer’s sticky weather behind us, fall’s coolness means those bangs you’ve been dying to get won’t get too stuck to your forehead. “Being in a humid environment, they can devastate you in the summer, as the humidity makes them fussy. A fringe or bang can make you look styled and undone at the same time, meaning you can just style your bangs and pull your hair up to look chic. I recommend a fuller style rather than wispy so they look intentional. Keep hairline, cowlicks, and recession in mind, as bangs can have a mind of their own; style them first, as they can be difficult to dry on their own. For a softer, fuller look, ask your stylist to point-cut the ends for softness,” Wright explains.

Davines This is a Dry Texturizing Finishing Spray

“For long, high-volume natural textures, go with Davines Dry Texturize. This enhances volume on the mid-shaft through ends, especially for those who like texture and volume on their ends. Clipping [the hair] back [is an easy way] to give high volume to a half-up/half-down look and secure it with a claw clip.”—Eric Marshall Wright

“Those with sleek or textured bobs, I personally would recommend the Davines Shimmering Mist, as it creates high shine with zero hold. This could really emphasize the texture of the bob whether it’s straight or blown out or done with waves.”—EMW

Davines Hair Refresher, Dry Cleansing Shampoo

“As far as bangs go, I highly recommend the Davines Hair Refresher because bangs can be tricky and you sometimes need to rewash them. It’s nice to have something that is powder base to refresh them, that way you’re not re-styling them or rewetting them—you’re just refreshing them.”—EMW

Featuring a fall-ready leopard design, Wright loves these clips for tightly securing long hair in half-up, half-down styles.

#6794. Your Houseplants Need a New Care Routine for Winter—Here Are 4 Easy Habits to Start

Give your indoor greenery a fighting chance until spring.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

With colder weather drawing our attention indoors, many of us swap outdoor garden maintenance for caring for our favorite houseplants. But while your spider plant, peace lily, or monstera may be safely tucked away inside, their needs change significantly during autumn and winter.

Reduced daylight, cooler temperatures, and drier indoor air can all affect the health of houseplants, meaning the care routine that worked perfectly in summer could actually do more harm than good during the colder months.

It turns out many of us are unknowingly making a few common mistakes that can leave indoor plants struggling through winter. Here are the habits to avoid, plus the simple swaps that will help keep your houseplants healthy until spring.

You might assume that moving your houseplant closer to a bright window is an excellent idea in winter to allow it to drink in as much sunlight as possible. And while this isn't necessarily a bad move, it just requires a little more thought.

“It’s always a good idea to move them closer to a bright window as darker days hit, but make sure to avoid cold drafts and radiators,” Mark Sage, B&Q's horticulture expert, tells us. “Cold drafts and dry air from radiators can all take their toll on indoor plants, leading to yellowing leaves, rotten roots or even plant fatality.”

Similarly, Sage suggests thinking about where the sun actually shines in your home throughout autumn and winter.

“Where natural light may be limited, consider investing in a plant light to give an extra boost, while rotating larger plants daily can help avoid leggy, awkward growth as they stretch out looking for the sunshine,” he suggests.

When houseplants start to look a little worse for wear and growth begins to slow over winter, it's tempting to step up your care routine. You might find yourself watering more often, tending to leaves more regularly, or even feeding plants in an attempt to encourage new growth.

However, Sage warns that this is often the wrong approach. In fact, during autumn and winter, “sometimes the best thing you can do for your plants is leave them alone!”

He explains: “In a slower growth period, plants generally need less water and feeding, and winter certainly isn’t the time for regular repotting.”

Instead, Sage recommends letting the soil dry out between waterings to help prevent root rot and giving plants time to rest. “It's worth giving them a boost of plant feed ahead of the winter months, but a little goes a long way,” he says. “Give plants their final dose at the end of autumn.”

During summer, when houseplants are actively growing, it's common to spread them throughout your home so you can enjoy a touch of greenery in different rooms. However, according to Sage, this can work against them once temperatures drop.

Instead, he recommends bringing your plants together during autumn and winter to help recreate the conditions many tropical houseplants naturally prefer.

“Give your houseplants a helping hand through winter by clustering them together to create a more humid microclimate, as many of these plants originally come from much hotter parts of the world,” he recommends. “Grouping several varieties close together can help maintain humidity around them, which can be particularly useful when central heating makes indoor air drier.”

While watering may have been your main plant-care task during summer, it's a mistake to neglect cleaning your houseplants over autumn and winter. Just like the rest of your home, plants can gather dust, and that build-up can make it harder for them to make the most of limited daylight.

“During winter, leaves can gather a layer of dust,” says Sage. “Give them a wipe down every one to two weeks with a microfibre cloth to support photosynthesis, and to help them benefit from even the smallest burst of sunlight.”

In fact, Sage suggests combining cleaning and watering occasionally by giving your plants a gentle shower.

“Use lukewarm water to boost humidity and get rid of any pests, but keep an eye on the temperature as if it is too cold you can shock the roots, but too warm and you can scald the leaves.”

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6793. We’ve Tested Virtually Every At-Home Laser Hair Removal Device—These 9 Stood Out

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

See the best picks of 2026, how they work, the results, and the deals you shouldn't miss.

Here’s my unfiltered review of laser hair removal: It’s painful, expensive, and takes a lot of sessions to see results. Soon, I'm running right back to waxing (ouch) and shaving (annoying). I thought at-home laser hair removal devices would be an expensive little beauty gadget that did nothing—yet another tool piled up next to my abandoned skincare devices. But I was so wrong. They're actually the best of both worlds.

An at-home laser hair removal device actually can help reduce the number of hairs that grow on you by damaging the follicle with every zap—all from your bed, couch, bathroom, wherever. This makes your hair look sparser, creating the illusion that you have far less hair (and making hair removal a whole lot quicker). And yep, at-home laser hair removal devices do work. They just use lower energy levels than the professional devices at a medspa or dermatologist’s office, which means they also require way more treatments to get similar results.

I’ve tried out all the top devices on the market for two months, evaluating how easy they were to use, how long they take on different areas of the body, the level of pain, and the efficacy, all to come up with this list of the top 10 out there. Here’s a peek at our top favorites.

Keep reading for a bit more info on these devices, plus a full review of all of our picks (like how we use them, the results we've seen in two months, and more).

Safety Guidelines and Skin Type Considerations:

Annoyingly, there are currently no at-home laser hair removal devices that are safe to use on dark skin and/or light hair, even if the device claims to work for “everyone.” In reality, they won’t work—or are unsafe for—any skin tones deeper than “light brown” or on hair colors lighter than “medium brown.” So make sure to speak with your dermatologist before wasting your money or, worse, damaging your skin. Got it? Good.

Now keep reading for info on how at-home laser hair removal works, the difference between IPL devices and laser devices, and first-person reviews on the best ones to try.

Why we love it: After four months of consistent testing, this Philips IPL device quickly rose in the ranks as the best laser hair removal device we’ve tried. It’s seriously high-tech, which can sometimes make something more complicated, but in this instance, it ups the beginner-friendly factor by miles. Once you connect the device to the accompanying app, the device actually speaks to you as you’re using it. So as you glide it down your leg or under your arms, the device will say if you’re doing it correctly or if you missed a spot.

Each attachment is geared toward a different body area (body—like arms and legs—face, bikini, and underarms), and they also use smart sensors to adjust the treatment to your skin’s needs, taking into account your skin tone and hair color. But the key to IPL hair removal is consistency, though, and the app actually tracks your progress and will send you a notification when you’re due for another treatment.

Cosmo editor's review: "I actually flew to Amsterdam in June to get a first sneak peek before this was available in America last month. I’ve been testing it ever since, and it has truly changed my hair removal entirely. I’m no stranger to laser hair removal devices; I’ve actually been using them on and off for six years. But this worked the fastest of anything I’ve tried. In just two treatments, spaced two weeks apart, I noticed less hair under my arms. Now, I only use it once a month or so, and my hair barely grows. I realized I have a pile of shaving creams to test, and I haven’t used a single one yet.

But the results aren’t the only thing that’s kept me consistent. I also just love how easy this is to use. Especially on areas like my bikini line, where I can’t really see what I’m doing, the device telling me that I’m doing it right—and when I’m doing it wrong—has absolutely made a difference. I only just started using this on my bikini line, as I was waiting until summer was over to avoid any potential irritation, but I’m quickly seeing some promising results. ”—BETH GILLETTE, BEAUTY EDITOR

Why we love it: A handful of Cosmo editors have tested the Ulike laser hair removal devices over the years, and every single person has shared rave reviews. And after testing five different devices on myself (doing hard work over here), I can confidently say the Air 3 model is the best one. It's affordable, extremely easy to use and set up, doesn't hurt, and comes with everything you need (including a mini razor to shave with before starting). Lemme get into it:

First of all, Ulike uses a unique "ice cooling" technology that essentially cools down your skin as it zaps you, cutting down on any pain and making your treatment really comfortable. To make it easy to use, the device has three different modes: "Soft mode" for your face and upper lip, "body mode" for your arms and legs, and "power mode" for your under arms and crotch—all of which are designed to work the best on these areas based on the thickness of skin and hair (think about it: your upper lip doesn't need the same power as your pubes). And when you actually use the tool, you can stamp it against your skin and press the button, or glide it across and it'll auto-zap you (which definitely trims treatment length).

Cosmo editor's review: "I'm no stranger to laser hair removal devices and have used a handful over the years. I really didn't think I could love one this much, though (I should have listened to my coworkers, lol). I've been putting this to use on my underarms where my skin is sensitive and gets irritated so easily. And I've never noticed any issues. Best of all, though, it's so easy to use that I've actually remained consistent. I am quick to stop doing things once they become complicated (I'm a Sagittarius! I can't help it!), but all I have to do is shave, plug this in, turn it on, and start zappin'. There aren't any useless buttons to press or apps to connect to. And the key to getting the results you want—i.e. fewer hairs over time—is keeping up with your treatments. I've used this now for two months, and I definitely am picking up the razor less. Now, I pretty much only shave when I'm going to do a treatment."—BG

Why we love it: Like the Dyson of laser hair removal devices, the Braun Skin i-Expert won the Cosmo Holy Grail Beauty Award in2024. The device pairs with a free app to take you through the whole process, so it's fantastic if you're totally new to this whole thing and need some guided help. The app also keeps track of all of your treatments so you know how often you're using it to really nab your results. There are also two methods based on different areas of your skin: A stamping method that's great for getting more precise areas, and a gliding method that you can use on your legs or underarms.

It comes with a lot (which certainly helps make up for the price). There are four heads (two precision caps for face and your bikini line, one wide cap for your body, and one standard size, all of which have skin tone and hair color sensors to avoid any potential skin damage), plus a Gillette Venus razor—aka a very good one—to shave with beforehand and a chic carrying case to store everything in.

FWIW, this Braun device is highly loved on Reddit, especially by users with tan to medium skin tones who normally find they can’t use most IPL devices without damaging their skin. One reviewer said they love that the device automatically adjusts the settings based on their skin tone, which tends to be on the low side for their medium skin. They said they used it twice weekly for five months and noticed that they barely have any hair on their underarms, legs, and bikini line these days, and even the hair that does grow comes back very slowly. So while, yes, it’s an investment, it’s a whole lot more ~fancy~ than the others on this list—and still a fraction of what you’d spend on laser hair removal treatments in-office, FYI.

One Cosmo editor's review: "I was a laser hair removal virgin, except when I tried getting my under-arms professionally lasered in high school, but my pain tolerance was too low to do more than two sessions, so I was super excited to try an at-home laser device. I loathe shaving, so I'll usually use an epilator or wax my underarms. I will say upon first impression, I loved how Braun's device came with allll the bells and whistles—including a razor. The reminders on my phone really held me accountable. The app also guided me through treatments that felt pretty quick, which made me way more likely to do them. And I especially love that I can just stuff everything back in the (tbh, very chic) carrying case to hide amongst my other beauty gadgets."—BG

This at-home laser utilizes intense pulsed light (IPL) to help gradually reduce hair growth, and I’ve noticed a significant difference, especially on my underarms and legs.

We’ve been testing at-home laser hair removal devices for five years, bringing you the best ones with all of our firsthand experience and updating whenever we try new launches or underrated gems. To compile this full list, we:

Laser hair removal is a process where specific wavelengths of light are directed onto your skin in little pulses, targeting and heating up the hair follicle to destroy a portion of it with each treatment, says dermatologist Arash Akhavan, MD. Compared to in-office treatments (which, although pricey, are done by board-certified derms or trained professionals who know exactly what they’re doing), at-home devices have a much lower energy output—mostly because they often use IPL (intense pulsed light) rather than true laser technology—which makes them safer for you to use on your own, says dermatologist Julie Russak, MD.

Yes, at-home laser-hair removal devices do work, but they take consistency. Because at-home devices use lower energy levels than in-office treatments, they require continued use (think: weekly or every time you shave) to notice similar results, says cosmetic doctor Ana Mansouri, MD. Basically, they are not for the lazy.

No, laser hair removal isn’t necessarily permanent (even though that’s what they told us). Despite how they sound, these devices don’t permanently remove hair in a few sessions, leaving you hairless like a full-body wax. Instead, laser-removal devices only help to reduce the total number of hair follicles in your skin and potentially make your existing hair finer and thinner (only electrolysis hair removal can permanently remove all hair, FYI). Even in-office laser hair removal isn’t completely permanent. Typically, you’ll need to go in once every 8 to 12 weeks after you’ve done your round of treatments (which is usually between 6 to 12, depending on the area, your hair, etc.)

Neither IPL nor laser-hair removal is “better”—it really just comes down to preference. True laser hair removal only uses one wavelength of light (essentially, one precise laser beam) that targets just the hair follicle to help slow down hair growth. IPL (intense pulsed light), on the other hand, isn’t actually a laser. Instead, they emit broad-spectrum light in multiple wavelengths that spread across a larger area of skin, meaning they are less precise and won’t penetrate your skin as deeply. That’s why IPL devices are generally gentler and easier for people to use, which is why most at-home devices use IPL rather than laser beams.

That said, both IPL and laser-hair removal can slowly destroy your hair follicle to reduce hair growth over time with continued use. But expect laser-hair removal to be a bit stronger (and potentially more painful or irritating), which is why it often yields quicker and better results. Meanwhile, IPL devices are usually not as intense, so they’re great for using consistently at home or for use on sensitive skin, like on your bikini line or face.

Do dermatologists recommend at-home laser hair removal?

Yes, it’s okay to do laser-hair removal at home, according to Dr. Akhavan, but there are some things to consider. “At-home devices tend to be generally safe, though a real potential for adverse effects, like scarring, does exist,” he says. Which is why most dermatologists will suggest opting for the professional in-office route, if you have the means (laser hair removal can cost anywhere from $200 to $1,500 and usually requires at least six sessions spaced six weeks apart).

Just note: “If you’re using an at-home device, don’t use it on sensitive areas, like your face and neck,” says Dr. Akhavan. “And if you have sensitive skin, always patch-test first on a smaller area, then wait three to four weeks to make sure there are no complications.” And depending on the area you’re treating, you may also need to adjust your skincare routine to exclude skin-thinning ingredients (like chemical exfoliants or retinol serums) for three days before and after your laser-hair removal treatment.

Does at-home laser hair removal work on dark skin?

Currently, at-home laser-hair removal devices don’t truly work for dark skin. These lasers work by targeting the dark color of your hair follicle, and at-home devices don’t have the technology yet to properly distinguish between dark hair follicles and dark skin, meaning they can potentially burn you. While some devices might market themselves as being safe for dark skin, they are usually considering medium-to-tan skin versus actual deep-brown skin tones.

So far, there are no at-home devices that are safe to use on true type-six Fitzpatrick scale skin tones (reminder: This dermatological scale ranges from one to six, with six being “brown to dark-brown” skin). Most laser-hair removal devices are only approved for up to level-four skin tones, with four being “olive to medium” skin. But if you’re unsure where your skin tone lies, talk to your dermatologist before you try an at-home laser device.

What happens if you don’t shave before at home laser hair removal?

Yes, you should shave about 24 hours before using an at-home laser-hair removal device, otherwise the IPL/laser won’t be able to correctly target the melanin (pigment) in your hair follicle. This could potentially cause discomfort, like redness, swelling, and irritation. Because these devices work by homing in on dark, contrasting colors (think: a black dot on a white canvas), they need to be able to differentiate between your skin and your follicle to prevent errors or burns. FYI: Even stubble isn't okay for laser hair removal because it will create too much heat and can damage your skin (plus, it won’t even work to help reduce the hair anyway).

Just remember: Don’t wax before your laser hair removal. Waxing completely rips out the hair and its root, which will leave nothing for the light to target, and you won’t be able to use your device. Instead, make sure your skin is freshly shaved and free of lotions or creams.

At-home laser hair removal can be slightly painful for some, but it all depends on your pain tolerance. Many people describe it as a light sting—like a rubber band snapping against your skin—but some people don’t even notice any pain at all. Your device’s strength and intensity levels will also dictate how painful the treatment will be. In general, though, most people say it’s relatively comfortable when done at home (I would give it a 1 out of 10—it’s a piece of cake, IMO).

For the most part, yes, you can use at-home laser hair removal devices on your bikini line. But you should be very careful. This should only ever be done externally on your vulva. And keep in mind how sensitive the skin in your pubic region is. Generally, it's best to opt for the lightest setting, and go slow with the device. Of all of the at-home IPL devices we tested, our favorite for pubic hair was the NOOD Flasher Pro.

It's best to skip lasering until your skin has cleared if you're dealing with razor bumps. Reminder: Razor bumps are the tiny red bumps you get on your skin after shaving or waxing when the hair strand grows back into the skin instead of out. They're usually inflamed and irritated, which is why it's ideal to just let them heal and swipe on a soothing treatment for a few days before lasering.

If you’re just starting out with at-home laser hair removal, there can be a slight learning curve. Keep these in mind:

Like I said above, you have to shave beforehand to see any reduction in hair, and it’s usually recommended to shave right before. Usually, I’ll exfoliate in the shower the day before with a body scrub, then 24 hours later, I’ll shave and use my laser-hair removal device on clean, dry skin. Exfoliating the day before helps cut down on some irritation but still ensures a close shave.

While laser hair removal is typically well-tolerated, it can potentially make your skin a little inflamed and irritated. To offset this, always finish your treatments with a moisturizing body treatment, whether that’s a body lotion, body serum, or rich body butter. And if you’re prone to ingrown hairs, try incorporating an exfoliating treatment once or twice a week, like a chemical exfoliant pad or serum.

Remember: Laser hair removal takes time. One treatment won’t do much, and neither will a couple. It takes up to three months to start noticing a reduction in hair. Stay consistent (this usually means treating your skin once a week, but it depends on your skin type).

Only some devices are suitable for deep skin tones, and even fewer can work on light hair, so read the description of the product carefully, then scan the reviews for people who have a similar skin tone or hair color as you and see how they rate the device. We noted above if a device can be used on lighter hair, fyi.

You’ll also want to consider the size of the device you’re purchasing and the area that you intend to use it on, says Dr. Mansouri. Basically, a tiny device meant for your face probably isn’t going to be the best if you’re looking for a tool to quickly target your legs.

Do you want a device you can take on the go? Then you should definitely opt for something cordless (The only one we tested that we loved was by Tria Beauty, FYI). Or if you want something that’s going to be really easy to use, try the Braun Skin-I Expert, which connects to an app to help you track your treatments. Another loved feature by testers? A cooling effect to help bring down potential discomfort and pain, like the Ulike IPL device.

#6791. The 16 Best New Hotels in New York City

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

In the last five years, the Big Apple has seen some of its biggest openings and major renovations in history. These are the best of the bunch.

Visiting New York City makes for a jam-packed trip. From shows on Broadway to walks through Central Park, from exhibits at the museums (Met? MoMA? Take your pick!) to shopping Fifth Avenue, days here are booked from start to finish. Of course, it’s then that the real fun starts—Manhattan is full of the best bars and restaurants in the world.

Given the length of the to-do list, some travelers think that where they stay while in town doesn’t matter. We disagree. Just because you won’t spend the bulk of your time lounging about in your hotel robe doesn’t mean you don’t need a plush option to coddle you after an afternoon spent zipping from sight to sight or a long night out on the tiles. A big bed, crisp linens, and a killer lobby bar can make a visit, after all.

New York City is a town obsessed with the new. (It’s even right there in the name: New York City.) And over the last five years it has seen an influx of hotels that are as much the destination as the line items on any itinerary. From the namesake brands that finally have a place to call home here, like the Faena, to the most iconic renovation of the decade at the Waldorf Astoria, here is exactly where you should be booking right now.

Faena shelters you from the city’s chaos, but it’s New York through and through. To the west, views of the Hudson River Greenway and Little Island. To the east, you can high-five people on the High Line from the terrace of the Living Room bar. The most N.Y.C. touch? The staff’s suits are nicer than yours. Rooms from $1,395. —Dave Holmes

On the hotel’s website, it says that “to tell the story of New York is, in some sense, to tell the story of the Waldorf Astoria.” For once, the marketing is correct. Like Manhattan, the hotel’s story is a tale of outsize real estate ambitions. A global standard for hospitality ever since the combination of the Waldorf Hotel and the Astoria Hotel in 1897, the property became the prize in the most expensive hotel sale ever in 2014. Three years later, the new owners closed it for renovations. The project cost $2 billion and took eight years, during which time the property was traded again. The wait was worth it. From the rooms to the restaurants (there are three) to the sprawling lobby, where Cole Porter’s piano anchors the room, to the Guerlain Wellness Spa, the hotel outright dazzles. It’s the talk of Manhattan. And word is it’s already back up for sale. Rooms from $1,295. —Madison Vain

An understated facade on Thompson Street hides the sexiest hotel in SoHo. The Manner is the first of the latest brand by Standard International, and its flagship location is codesigned by the Standard’s Verena Haller and Milanese architect Hannes Peer, so the whole place is Studio 54 stylish, yet comfortable. Speakeasy Sloane’s is a great place for a cocktail and—we swear—the best chicken nuggets in town, while the Apartment is a cozy common space to use as your living room. Check out the penthouse, modeled after Halston’s office, and dream of the party you’ll throw there. There’s no better center of operations for your next New York trip. Rooms from $799. —D.H.

Don’t let yourself get swept away by the history. An Art Deco gem built in the 1920s at 76th and Madison, the Surrey was a longtime landmark in New York’s glitziest neighborhood, playing host to the likes of JFK and Bette Davis. It closed after a century and, following a multiyear renovation, recently reopened as the first North American property under global luxury hotel group Corinthia. From the outside, the property could easily pass for a residential building. Inside, you’ll find 70 rooms and 30 suites, each flawless. Muted terra-cotta-colored walls, crisp linens, and walk-in marble showers make for plush surroundings. The spa is small but divine. And the lobby bar and restaurant, Casa Tua (of Aspen, Miami, and Paris notoriety), is one of the hardest-to-get tables in town. Movie stars, politicians, famous divorcées, influencers—everyone here is someone. Including you. Rooms from $1,000. —M.V.

The Fifth Avenue Hotel leans into what New Yorkers with good taste who live in small spaces know is the secret to making their apartments feel luxurious: Love every single piece of furniture, adore every square inch. Nothing is wasted. If you’re lucky, you’ll get something like the rooms and hallways of the Fifth Avenue Hotel. It’s like rich-aunt maximalism: Missoni-style upholstered chairs and couches, an armoire that’s actually a giant bar adorned with dragons and birds. The wallpaper looks as though it’s hand-painted. Lots of tapestries and tufted pink walls. Unlike in many N.Y.C. apartments, you’ll get a great night’s sleep here—the room feels pin-drop quiet, and the bathrooms are serene marble white. This is New York, but you might not leave the hotel. There’s in-room martini service, and downstairs is one of the city’s best new restaurants, the old-school-luxe Café Carmellini, as well as the Portrait Bar, home to some of the town’s best martinis (outside of your room). Rooms from $895 —K.S.

Hotels where you pay by the month can be dicier than a Vegas craps table. But Maison Hudson is the polar opposite. Located on the banks of the Hudson and at the fringe of the West Village, the property straddles a blurry line between ultra-luxurious hotel and supremely plush apartment. The minimum stay is a month, and the price starts at thirty g’s. Sounds absurdly high? Maybe. But when suites in N.Y.C. go for several thousand dollars per night, a stay at Maison Hudson can feel kind of like a good deal over time. Boy math! Okay, so say you hit it big on a stock investment/crypto/inheritance and want to live like a 0.01 percenter in New York’s coolest neighborhood. Here’s what you get: a chic, tastefully decorated, ridiculously massive thousand-square-foot living space, complete with heated bathroom floors, automatic shades, a fully stocked kitchen, and an in-unit washer/dryer. The concierge—who is really like your own personal assistant—will get you anything you could possibly desire. (Think concert tickets, a rare bottle of wine, an endangered animal to eat for dinner—kidding!) There’s an in-house spa that offers massages, facials, and antiaging treatments in a Zen-like setting. Marius, the French-inspired restaurant helmed by Michelin-star chef Sébastien Sanjou, feels intimate with only a handful of tables. While the eatery is open to the public, don’t worry: Maison Hudson guests get first priority for seatings, natch. Rooms from $32,500 per month —Daniel Dumas

The brand’s three Manhattan locations have long been the smart traveler’s pick for affordable city lodging, and now, like so many city people, the Arlo group has made the jump into Brooklyn. On the site of the former Hotel Williamsburg, the most eye-catching of the luxury hotels to hit the hip ’hood in the 2010s, Arlo Williamsburg is the exact right home base for a New York City getaway. Brooklyn buzzes outside each room’s floor-to-ceiling windows, while a rooftop pool and a bar and event space inside the Arlo’s landmark water tower offer views of the full sweep of Manhattan’s skyline. It seems so close you could touch it, but with all of Billyburg’s bars, restaurants, shops, and general people-watching just steps from the front door, you may decide to skip the quick trip on the L and stay local. The company’s focus on art and interesting programming has survived the trip across the river. And out here you can stand in the middle of your room, extend both arms, and not touch a wall. Try that in Manhattan. Rooms from $400 —D.H.

On one end of the travel-fantasy spectrum, there is the blissed-out, blue-skies-and-beaches vacation. The stuff postcards are made of. Nothing wrong with seeking that. Toward the other end is the kind where you imagine yourself as a neighborhood local. You tap into the rhythm of the vibrant daily life in the blocks around you and feel as if you’ve merged with a hipper, alternate-universe version of yourself. That’s what Nine Orchard will do for you.

We know the “hotels for locals” shtick, where there’s a lobby filled with folks on laptops. This is not that. Nine Orchard is more about creating the fantasy of residence. It starts with your room number. 4L, 3G, 8A. You might think you’ve stepped into someone’s apartment. Each room has an appreciation for the analog—the handmade ceramic lamps, the paintings from local artists, the wooden Ojas speakers that tune in to stations curated by DJ Stretch Armstrong and Devon Turnbull. You’ll want to hole up and write a memoir here.

Lying in a cloudlike king bed, facing downtown, with One World Trade Center glimmering in the distance, I watched the sunset, thirty-four stories in the sky, and thought, What a thing to experience in a city where you thought you’d seen it all. As a gleaming, fifty-story glass tower, the Ritz-Carlton New York NoMad offers that Ritz-Carlton tranquility, but the bars and restaurants here have made it an unlikely nightlife destination in a decidedly non-touristy hood. You’ll have a sanctuary in your room with a tub—rare in N.Y.C.—but there’s also a buzzy lobby bar, the José Andrés rooftop lounge Nubeluz as well as his Bazaar Meat on the second floor, the first-floor Mediterranean delight Zaytinya, and, in the summer, the vibrant fifth-floor terrace bar, Dahlia’s Garden. It’s an uncommon New York hotel combo, where you can be serene or all-out energetic. Rooms from approx. $1,500 —K.S.

You can’t talk about the Hotel Chelsea without checking off the names of its famous tenants and guests. The building, finished in 1884, was conceived as a kind of commune for musicians and novelists and has had a bohemian artistic energy since then. Mark Twain, Stanley Kubrick, Patti Smith, Andy Warhol, and Jimi Hendrix are just some of the notable folks who have graced this Queen Anne–style building over the past century. Today it might lack a raucous clientele, but that’s all for a better night’s sleep. Expect laid-back service, marble-and-brass bathrooms, animal-print furniture. From the pitch-perfect El Quijote, a Spanish restaurant that opened in 1930, to the iconic stained-glass windows and wrought-iron balconies, checking into the Chelsea feels like entering a time warp. Rooms from approx. $300 —K.S.

Want to see something beautiful? Come stand on the corner of Wall Street and Water in Lower Manhattan and stare at this gorgeous mass of concrete. The Paspaley family, Australians who were pioneers in the pearl industry, lovingly and painstakingly brought the beaux arts facade of the Wall Street Hotel to life over the course of several years. And what’s inside is even better. Spacious suites (for Manhattan!), oversize bathrooms (see previous parenthetical), a chic-meets-fun bar that draws locals and guests, plus an absolutely killer in-house French brasserie—this place doesn’t miss. Rooms from $465 —M.V.

You’ll quickly lose track of how many food and beverage options there are at the iconic London brand’s NoMad location. Rooftop bar, lobby bar, library (with bar service), a trendy Italian spot, a private table in a water tower, and a formal dining room for table-side service. All have their own vibe and are worthy of inspection, but only some are available to hotel guests. The rest are reserved for Ned members. But worry not; there’s plenty of fun to be had for those who book here. Rooms from $375 —M.V.

The committed traveler, it stands to reason, always looks for authentic local color. So a stay in New York’s TriBeCa neighborhood should naturally involve a few nights in a soaring, modernist hymn to the twenty-four-hour possibilities of twenty-first-century New York. Right? Non. Hotel Barrière Fouquet’s, which opened recently on Greenwich Street, whiffs unmistakably and quite charmingly of Paris. For more than a hundred years, Fouquet’s existed as one of the grandest of all Parisian café-restaurants. In 2006, it entered the hotel business when it was reopened with more than one hundred luxury rooms added by the Barrière Group above and around the original café. The New York hotel is the first U.S. venture of the group, which maintains properties in some of the most chichi resorts in Europe. Its inherent Frenchness is both obvious and lightly done. The staff offer a singsongy “Bonjour” when you pass them in the corridor. In the rooms, the furnishings are postmodern, curvy takes on deco, and the toile de Jouy–style wallpaper is about as French as you can get in room decor, until you stand up close and realize it’s actually composed entirely of cartoony New York street scenes. With a relatively low room count—just ninety-seven in total, including a two-story Grand Appartement Terrasse—the hotel feels quiet and intimate. There’s just a single low-key bar, the Brasserie Fouquet’s New York, and the Par Ici Café, which doubles as the breakfast spot and where, reassuringly, the prices have been imported from Paris along with the style. Rooms from $900 —Nick Sullivan

As with many Aces around the globe, the Brooklyn outpost is very much about building a cool community hangout the only way the Ace can. The purpose-built, brutalist structure of concrete and wood is home to a lobby with a buzzing bar anchoring all of the loungy furniture, where folks Zoom on laptops by day and old-fashioneds are being stirred come nightfall. Each month features different DJs spinning soul, and the Atrium on the second floor often hosts poetry readings and film screenings. The woodshop-chic rooms aren’t bad either. Rooms from $199 —Omar Mamoon

Welcome to la dolce vita, a place where Loro Piana cashmere lines the walls of your presidential suite, where custom scents fill the air, where fireplaces roar and Bellinis are handed out by waiters in white jackets. The first of several membership clubs/boutique hotels planned by Italian hospitality’s best-known ambassadors, Casa Cipriani—located on South Street in Lower Manhattan—has had glitzy New Yorkers jockeying for access ever since it opened in 2022. The pitch was a gamble (there’s a dress code, and prices are astronomical), but it turns out that a lot of luxury feels just right these days. Rooms from approx. $750 —M.V.

Beyond the modern extension of Penn Station, you’ll come across an undulating tower seemingly conjured out of nowhere in an area that was, just a few years ago, a kind of no-man’s-land. For the trek across Ninth Avenue, the Pendry epitomizes Cali-luxe: plush, earth-toned rooms where curved windows create nooks to dwell in and admire the new urban vista. Downstairs, it’s time for a mini bar crawl. Bar Pendry is a gold-leaf-adorned jewel box for a more clandestine drink, and Chez Zou is like a belle epoque garden complete with deft cocktails and a secret cheeseburger. Rooms from $725 —K.S.

#6788. Martine Rose Is the New Creative Director of Balenciaga

An appointment the fashion world is eager to celebrate

Fashion fans rejoice: Martine Rose has officially been appointed creative director of Balenciaga following yesterday’s exit of Pierpaolo Piccioli. This appointment represents a welcome departure from the circulation of familiar names for these creative director roles and symbolizes a shift back to an edgier Balenciaga established by Demna. She begins November 16.

Rose was rumored to be up for the job when it became available after Demna’s departure to Gucci in 2025. And it’s a homecoming; when Demna was appointed to Balenciaga as creative director in 2015, he brought Rose on as a menswear consultant. In her three years working there, she helped to shape the look of his very distinctive vision.

Rose was born in South London in 1980 to a British Jamaican family. Her mother was a nurse and her father was an accountant. She spent her childhood around slightly older relatives deeply immersed in ’90s rave subcultures. The symbolically rich clothing choices shaped her early aesthetic way more than any Paris runway could. She studied fashion design at Middlesex University, where she discovered an affinity with menswear. Rose began creating early, opening the small T-shirt line LMNOP with a friend in 2002. She launched her namesake label in 2007 and began showing under the umbrella of Lulu Kennedy’s fashion incubator program Fashion East in the early 2010s. That’s where she began gaining traction in niche fashion circles.

With her own collection, Rose elevated her memories of clubs—and the clashes of the different tribes within them—for a fashion audience, offering revisions of those shiny shirts, baggy pants, and anoraks. She is known for subverting archetypal garments, distorting proportions, and invoking unconventional fabrics like satin and lace. Her work nods to subcultural heroes who used fashion to convey identity, like the Lo Lifes and Dapper Dan. She can shrink a pair of pants just enough to be a little awkward and pair them with a sports jersey and loafers with ultra-extended toes in an ensemble that somehow makes total sense. Through her lens, the mundane becomes complex, thought-provoking, even extraordinary.

Demna caught on to what Rose was doing and brought her on to develop Balenciaga menswear following his appointment in 2015. She worked with him for three years and helped influence his highly recognizable men’s aesthetic, one that often featured the exaggerated shoulders, boxy cuts, and logo experimentation that she already favored. Her work at the luxury house brought attention to her own label, buoying its renown in fashion. She has since produced several high-profile collabs with brands like Nike, Supreme, and Clarks, and she counts Timothée Chalamet, Rihanna, and Hailey Bieber among her devotees.

Balenciaga is having a bit of an identity crisis. The couture house was founded by Cristóbal Balenciaga in 1917 in San Sebastián, Spain, and later reestablished in Paris in 1937. Often referred to as an “architect” of fashion rather than a designer, Cristóbal is remembered for a vision rooted in shape, volume, and silhouette. His creations were both sculptural and fluid, with iconic looks including the “tunic” and “sack” silhouettes. His legacy was carried on by Michel Goma, Alexander Wang, and most notably, Nicolas Ghesquière and Demna.

In 2025, amid an industrywide reshuffling of creative directors, Balenciaga appointed Pierpaolo Piccioli to its helm, tasking him with interpreting the house’s legacy of sculptural elegance without alienating the streetwear superfans Demna courted. It was a difficult job; Piccioli left just 16 months later. Rose’s appointment shows that the house is looking to reignite some of Demna’s subversive edge. But zooming out, her return is undeniably a celebratory one; she is beloved by fashion and streetwear fans alike. It feels in alignment with the excitement around Grace Wales Bonner’s appointment to Hermès. The current roster of creative directors at big luxury houses is mostly white and male; these are two women of color whose work has been quietly shaping the fashion landscape without always getting their due.

#6787. Casio’s New Mechanical Field Watch Is a Game-Changer

The affordable titanium timepiece puts the Japanese brand squarely in the consideration set for budding collectors.

Since forever, Casio has dominated the market for affordable digital and quartz watches. Now it's making a play for the wallet-friendly end of the mechanical watch market, territory traditionally dominated by fellow Japanese manufacturers Seiko, Citizen, and Orient.

The new CMK-100 is Casio's first automatic watch to carry its mainline branding, following the introduction of mechanical models under its Edifice label last year. And on paper, it's a pretty compelling proposition.

Inspired by classic military field watches—with more than a passing resemblance to the Rolex Explorer and the Tudor Ranger—the CMK-100 has a 38mm titanium case, sapphire crystal, screw-down crown, and 100 meters of water resistance. Its textured dial features luminous Arabic numerals, while a Japanese automatic movement provides a 42-hour power reserve. The movement's manufacturer hasn't been disclosed, although its specifications suggest it could be a Miyota 8215, a calibre used by Timex, Spinnaker, and Invicta, among others.

There are five versions: one on a titanium bracelet, two on nylon straps, and two more on Italian leather straps—including a rather jazzy brown-dial model with a rose gold-colored case. Prices range from $330 to $440.

For Seiko, which has built a substantial business selling affordable mechanical watches through collections such as the Seiko 5 Sports, Casio's arrival in this category represents an interesting challenge. Titanium cases and sapphire crystals are hardly standard equipment at this price, and Casio already has an enormous following among younger watch buyers.

Whether those customers will embrace a mechanical from the home of G-Shock remains to be seen. But for anyone shopping for a good-looking, affordable automatic watch, there's now another Japanese name in the running for your attention.

#6786. 14 Everyday Home Items You Need to Replace More Often Than You Think

These overlooked swaps can make your home cleaner, safer, and fresher.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Your home might be a showcase of timeless design, with stylish accessories, custom artwork, and high-end furniture. But when the everyday things that keep a household functioning are left unattended to for long enough, the space may be less clean and organized (and safe!) than it looks.

“Even the most gorgeous spaces lose their charm if we forget to update essentials,” says Linda Hayslett, principal designer at LH.Designs. “Keeping your home looking and feeling fresh isn’t just about design—it’s also about the little things.”

Caring for your home means looking beyond the interiors. Giving neglected areas the attention they need can keep germs and grime at bay, and replacing certain items can also protect your health and safety. Consider your carbon monoxide alarm: If an aging unit fails during an emergency, the consequences could be deadly.

To help you keep everything in good shape, we asked experts which 14 household items you should replace as soon as possible. Take a look—you can thank us later!

Smoke and carbon monoxide detectors lose their sensitivity over time, which could delay detection during an emergency. The timeframe for when you should replace these will vary by manufacturer. Look up the model you have online to be sure—even a fairly new alarm should be tested once a month. The batteries should be replaced once a year.

Mold and mildew can build up on shower liners, especially in damp environments. A 2024 study by SafeHome.org showed that shower curtains had the most germs of any other surfaces in the bathroom, including the toilet seat. While Hayslett recommends washing them every three months, the material can eventually become brittle over time.

Dingy towels are the antithesis of luxe. Towels lose their absorbency and softness after years of washing.

“Avoid using too much detergent, which can build up on fibers,” Hayslett says. “Use a little vinegar during the rinse cycle to keep towels soft.” If your bath towels feel scratchy or start to smell musty despite washing, it’s time for a refresh.

To prolong the life of your towels, make sure you let them dry properly between uses to prevent mildew growth, noted Vyanca Soto of Market Studio Interiors. And you should definitely swap them out if you notice discoloration from your skincare products.

You likely spend plenty of time in your kitchen and bathroom each day. Make sure you take a few minutes to examine the grout lines around your bathtub and kitchen countertops.

“Grout breaks down over time, and you can end up with water damage to the drywall,” says Rebekah Zaveloff, cofounder and creative director at Imparfait Design Studio. “If you look and there are spots that have pulled away or deteriorated, it’s time to regrout.”

A sagging mattress is a clear sign it’s time for a new one. Mattresses lose support and can accumulate dust mites, allergens, and even mold, which is certainly not a dreamy way to slumber.

“Flip your mattress every six months [unless it's a one-sided design] to help maintain its structure and extend its lifespan,” Hayslette advised.

A mattress protector can extend its lifespan as does regular vacuuming to remove dust and debris. Soto recently bought a bed vacuum cleaner and says, “It works like a charm.” She also uses it on her dog’s bed to help it stay clean and smell fresher.

The lifespan of a pillow mainly depends on its material, whether that be foam, feathers, or another type of synthetic fill. But once a pillow of any kind loses its shape, it's time to say goodbye. Like mattresses, pillows can harbor dust mites, dead skin cells, and sweat over time. You can prolong a pillow's life with a pillow protector—Soto likes the Serta Power Chill—but you should still swap those out every year or so.

When to replace: two to three years for sheets; five to 10 years for duvets

Bed linen care and personal hygiene dictate the condition of your linens. Similar to pillows, sheets and duvets can accumulate dust mites, allergens, and bacteria, especially if you allow kids or pets into your bed.

“Over time, duvets lose their fluffiness and insulating properties due to regular use and washing,” says Rebecca Bobroff of Rebecca Bobroff Design.

If you’re prone to night sweats or use a lot of skin-care products, you may notice that your sheets become quickly discolored. Along with regular washing, you may want to stick to white sheets you can bleach over colors, which will look dingy even when they're clean.

“At a minimum, wash pillowcases once a week,” says Danielle Hurhula, head of home merchandising for Quince. “I’d recommend bumping washes up to twice a week if you suffer from allergies or asthma, sleep with hair or skincare products, or have sensitive or acne-prone skin.”

HVAC maintenance experts, including Energy Star, recommend checking filters monthly and replacing air filters every three months to help your system function properly and keep dust and allergens out of your home.

If you remove your filters and they look extra dirty, you may want to up the frequency. Doing so will not only improve indoor quality but keep your HVAC system from overworking and wasting energy, which could shave a few years off its life.

Water filters can accumulate bacteria, minerals, and other contaminants. This can affect the taste and quality of your drinking water.

“The lifespan of a filter cartridge is influenced by the level of contaminants and particulates in your water,” says Eric Roy, Ph.D., founder and chief scientist at Hydroviv. “Sink filter cartridges should be replaced approximately every six months and are designed to handle up to 720 gallons of water.”

You should also watch for a decrease in the flow rate, which indicates the filter is saturated.

When to replace: five years (or as recommended by the manufacturer)

You likely know that fridge water filters should be replaced every six months, but water lines can build up gunk or start leaking over time.

“Inspect the line for cracks or discoloration every year,” Soto suggests. “Flush it with vinegar and water every six months to prevent mineral buildup and ensure clean, clear water. My mom taught me this and when you see the color of the water when you flush it—you'll never stop doing it.”

Using sharp knives on wooden and plastic cutting boards can create grooves that embed bacteria, even after a dishwasher cycle. One study showed that plastic surfaces have the longest bacteria survival time, and scraping wooden boards with a knife significantly reduced bacterial presence.

Soto disinfects cutting boards with a vinegar solution and uses bleach for plastic boards. “Sand down wooden boards occasionally to remove deep grooves and re-oil them to maintain the wood,” she says.

Sponges harbor bacteria, especially when you use them to clean up raw food such as eggs and meat. In fact, salmonella can thrive on a sponge for more than seven days. Hayslette’s tip: Microwave damp sponges for one minute to sanitize between uses, but toss them after two weeks max.

Keep sponges as dry as possible, or better yet, replace them with dish brushes as these are less likely to breed bacteria, according to the International Journal of Food Microbiology. We like the Curio dish brush because it stands upright on a magnetic pedestal and you can replace the brush heads.

When to replace: three to five years (or sooner if scratched)

The nonstick coating on your cookware can wear off or become damaged over time. This decreases a pan's efficacy and also makes it a health hazard—the coating may start to flake off into your food.

“To extend their life, always use nonmetal utensils and hand-wash your pans instead of placing them in the dishwasher,” Hayslette says.

Not only do latex anti-slip rug pads lose cushioning over time, but they can also start to disintegrate.

“They get tacky and sticky on the floor, which can damage the floor's surface,” Zaveloff explains. Pull up your rug once in a while and if you see that the rug pads are turning dark yellow, start shopping for new ones.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6783. Seiko’s Affordable Hideo Kojima Watches Are a Gamer's Dream—but There's a Catch

The Japanese brand has teamed up with Kojima Productions to celebrate its 10th anniversary with two limited-edition timepieces.

If you're a fan of Hideo Kojima's video games, you might want to take a look at Seiko's later collaboration. The watchmaker has teamed up with Kojima Productions, the studio behind Death Stranding, to release two limited-edition watches celebrating its 10th anniversary, both exclusive to Japan.

And, in keeping with Kojima's rather distinctive creative universe, these aren't your typical Seikos.

The new Ludens watches come in two colourways, black and white, with just 300 of each available. Both take inspiration from Ludens, the futuristic, spacesuit-wearing mascot of Kojima Productions, whose illustration takes pride and place on the dial alongside the studio's motto From Sapiens to Ludens.

The white edition features a silver-and-white dial paired with a patterned white strap, while the black version offers a darker alternative. Turn either watch over and you'll find an engraved illustration of Ludens on the case back, alongside the Seiko logo and an individual serial number.

As for what's inside, both watches are powered by Seiko's battery-operated 6N01 quartz movement, which offers an accuracy of approximately 15 seconds per month and a battery life of around three years. There's also a stainless steel case, Hardlex crystal, and everyday water resistance.

The black edition is priced at ¥55,000 (approximately $350), while the white version costs ¥57,600 (around $365). The latter also comes with a ticket to Kojima Productions' Beyond the Strand 10th-anniversary exhibition, taking place at Tokyo's Azabudai Hills Gallery until December 20.

Pre-orders opened in Japan on October 30 through Lawson and HMV&BOOKS, with reservations running until December 14, unless all 300 pieces sell out first.

But here's the catch. Despite being available to pre-order now, neither watch is expected to arrive until September 30 2027. That's almost eleven months of waiting for a watch that's already rather difficult to get hold of, particularly if you're shopping from outside Japan.

Still, for diehard Kojima fans, a limited-edition Seiko celebrating a decade of one of gaming's most recognizable studios might just be worth the wait.
