#6782. All About Paige Bueckers and Azzi Fudd’s Friendship, Relationship, and Eventual Breakup

Bad news, sports fans: Paige Bueckers and Azzi Fudd have called it quits...and apparently, the breakup happened a while ago. But thankfully, it seems like the two are still on good terms. During a recent appearance on Azzi’s podcast, Paige confirmed their split happened “over a year” ago, noting that it happened before Azzi was drafted to the Dallas Wings. Ugh, my heart!

In case you somehow missed all the signs, the former UConn basketball teammates met as teens when they were both trying out for point guard on the USA Basketball U16 team, and over the years, their chemistry went from courtside to heart-eyes. And while they may no longer be together, at least we’ll always have their friendship, right??

Ahead, Paige Bueckers and Azzi Fudd’s full relationship timeline for your scrolling pleasure.

According to The UConn Blog, Paige and Azzi first met when they were teenagers trying out for a USA Basketball team at summer camp. At the time, Azzi described Paige as “this skinny little white girl” on the court. “I watched her play and I was like, ‘Oh, I have nothing to worry about this girl,’” Azzi said. “Like, ‘I have a better chance of making it than her.’”

After some friendly competition at USA Basketball, the pair formed a bond, and Paige reportedly started visiting Azzi in Washington, D.C. to work out together.

While Paige had already secured a spot on the UConn women’s basketball team, she supported Azzi and started a social campaign to get her on the team, too. Okay, P.B., we see you.

All of Paige’s work recruiting Azzi worked, because she committed to UConn in November 2020.

Shortly after, rumors that they lived together during the COVID-19 pandemic surfaced, thanks to a since-deleted TikTok of the pair bickering about getting sick.

Azzi and Paige are officially teammates! Paige shared a sweet sentiment about their dynamic on and off the court with The UConn Blog in 2021.

“I think we both hate losing but I think we both hate losing to each other more than anything. So we both want to win, we both want to make each other better,” Paige said. “I think that works on and off the court as well. I think we’re just here to make each other better and give each other opportunities and it’ll be fun to go at it in practice but once we get on the same court together and go against other teams, I think that’ll be good.”

#6781. According to Judi Dench, the Biggest James Bond Baddie Was Daniel Craig All Along

It turns out he was behind some of the last few Bond films' most shocking moves.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Daniel Craig’s much-celebrated time as 007 wasn’t just bad news for the world’s supervillains—the good guys dropped like flies, too.

After Craig joined the franchise in 2006’s Casino Royale, the legendary Judi Dench, who had played M since 1995’s GoldenEye, lasted just three films before kicking it. And as we all know—spoiler alert, just in case you haven’t seen the end of Craig’s 007 journey—Bond himself met an explosive end in No Time to Die. It turns out you can thank Craig for both plot twists.

Craig has been vocal about his desire to kill off 007 in the past, but in her new memoir, titled Is It Too Late to Make a Run for It?, Dame Judi Dench shared that he also had a hand in her own character's demise.

"I was taken to the Wolseley restaurant by Barbara [Broccoli], Michael [G. Wilson] and Sam [Mendes]—I don't think Daniel Craig was there—and they outlined the plot and told me they were going to bump me off," she revealed.

But the crucial new information is this: "Apparently, it was Daniel's idea to have me killed," Dame Dench revealed.

Ruthless stuff, but in fairness, it did result in one of the series's most impactful moments. Will the next Bond actor—which, of course, is an extremely hot conversation at the moment—get the same opportunity to decide his co-stars' fates? You would not have thought so. The next M, whoever it may be, can breathe a big sigh of relief.

#6780. Fine Form

Sculptural silhouettes, cinched waists, and sheer lace: fall’s dramatic new take on glamour is equal parts restraint and romance

#6777. Princess Kate’s Chic Outdoorsy Look Takes a Cue From Princess Diana

And other British royals who came before her

Trends may come and go, and generations may change, but one thing you can count on is that royal traditions will live on.

For years, the British royals have been dressing for England’s foggy, rainy weather, and for many of those years, they’ve reached to Barbour for appropriate outerwear.

The heritage brand, based in South Shields, England, is known primarily for its preppy outdoorsy jackets. And royal family fans have included none other than Queen Elizabeth II and Princess Diana. Inspired by their wardrobes, Princess Catherine and even Duchess Meghan have adopted the label.

(The company has long held a Royal Warrant, which is “granted as a mark of recognition to people or companies who have regularly supplied goods or services” to the royals, per the family’s website.)

Some favorites of Kate include the waxed defence jacket in olive, the Barbour x Alexa Chung Elfie coat in olive and khaki, the puffer-style Longshore quilted jacket in navy and marigold, and other diamond-quilted styles.

Today, in a series of photos shared on Instagram, the Princess of Wales wore the Alexa Chung collab style in sand and chocolate, layering it over a chunky navy sweater with a ribbed neck, and styling it with light-wash blue jeans.

To further elevate the look, she popped on some dangly diamond earrings and her diamond-and-sapphire engagement ring from husband Prince William (which once belonged to Lady Di).

She wore the ensemble to visit pioneering mental health charity A Dose of Nature. And it’s certainly one fit for a future queen of England.

#6774. MobLand Season 2, Episode 8 Takes a Devastating Turn

Conrad’s grip on reality is beginning to slip.

Last week I wrote a giddy hagiography of Frankie Cooper. It’s embarrassing how much I like him. I really should grow up, be a man, maintain some critical distance, and write in wry asides like a real English journalist.

But I can’t help it. He’s such an irresistibly murderous bastard that he makes me want to kill a cat just to see how it feels. (The lawyers have advised me to clarify that I don’t actually want to kill a cat; no cats were harmed in the making of this opinion.)

Well, Frankie, it’s been lovely. But there’s a new psychopath in town. Of course the Medusa had a vengeful son by the name of “Mad Dog”! Just out of prison for murder, he’s seen CCTV footage of Eddie, who was there when his daddy was killed, and he’s coming to the Cotswolds.

“I’ll find them, Daddy,” says Kelly, pouring a bottle of whisky over his father’s coffin.

But all that’s for episode 9. We’ve got the admin of episode 8 to sort through first.

Remember Harry’s words at the end of episode 7? “Is that Michael Cooper? At 3 a.m. you’re gonna get clapped.”

Well, Harry did indeed have Frankie Cooper’s boy killed. But not quite so easily as we were first led to believe. He wanted to fuck with Frankie in the process. Harry knew that Michael would call his dad, who has Newgate Prison sewn up tighter than a dodgy tailor’s pocket. Frankie therefore arranged for Michael to be moved to the solitary wing, where he would be safe.

But as Harry explains to Kevin: “What Frankie’s forgot is that a bent screw is a bent screw… So if the CM’s [custodial manager] gonna take a tenner, what’s he gonna do for a twenty? Or in this case, 200 grand in a black bag in the dead of night on a pissing-down Thursday.”

Harry then pays a couple of hired goons to string Michael up in his cell. Frankie is not going to be happy.

It’s time we gave Emmett J. Scanlan his flowers . He’s been ticking over in the background, doing a solid, likable job as the Harrigan dogsbody Paul. But he is changing now and emerging like a butterfly into the show’s comic sideline. There’s a real touch of the Baldricks about him, with a stroke of Hamlet’s gravedigger—the loyal dogsbody who gets lumbered with the shitty jobs and stays funny in spite of it all.

The poor sod thinks he’s in for the noose after the Sicily debacle of episode 7. He’s begging for his life, so desperate that he thinks VIP tickets to Rod Stewart might save him. But no. They were never going to kill Paul. It was merely a simple lampoon. They want him to cut up Donnie’s stinking body in a shipping container.

So Paul strips down to his leopard-print pants and, swinging his hips to the Rolling Stones’ “Mr Charm,” sets about chopping Donnie into vacuum-packable “bricks.” It’s all very Tom Cruise in Risky Business, but with more blood and less hairbrush. Long live Paul.

Eddie’s sinking deeper into the soup—and it’s all of his own doing. If only he weren’t—in Kevin’s words—such a “lazy, spoilt, entitled little cunt.” Maeve finds him in the sauna. He tells her their “situation” isn’t going to work.

“Maeve, I fear our relationship is sailing into choppy water. You’re 80. I’m barely legal.”

Maeve feigns surprise. “This approach, this ploy. It’s depraved. And deeply optimistic.”

Conrad, Eddie explains, is going to “piss his kegs.” Suddenly, so is Eddie, but not out of laughter. “Please don’t tell him; I’ll do anything.”

She agrees, but only if Eddie apologizes. On his knees. To her phone camera. It’s all fun and games for Maeve. I suspect we haven’t seen the last of that video.

Jan agrees to meet Harry. She wants to come clean, to pour out her heart. She tries to explain that she had a brief affair with a man who was using her to get to Harry and Conrad. But Harry already knows.

“That’s my job,” he tells her. “To anticipate danger. Control outcomes. Minimize unwanted consequences.”

She asks him about killing. About the nightmares. He says he doesn’t have any. You get the feeling Harry is all out of nightmares. Then he offers to help Jan and Gina run. Forever.

“I can make that happen. Tonight,” he tells her. “You never have to see me again.”

“What about you?” she asks. “Does no part of you want that?”

For a moment, Harry almost seems human. Almost.

It’s clear now: Conrad is losing his marbles. He wanders into the woods, calling out for his dead dogs. Then he stops, surrounded by swirling mists and squawking crows, and seems to realize just how far he’s slipping. He summons Kevin to the Cotswolds, to which the latter reluctantly submits. Conrad tells his son he’s seen a doctor. He wants to talk succession plans. Kevin, astonished at being asked such a question, reels furiously through the options.

First, Eddie: “A lazy, entitled psychopath who I wouldn’t trust to light the barbecue.”

Then Seraphina: “She would make a world-class businesswoman, but a kingpin? I don’t think she’s ever even touched a gun.”

And then, just as Kevin is about to pitch himself, Conrad reveals what’s really going on. “I see the dead,” he hisses with a haunted, hounded look, the air of the ancient mariner. “They come to me at night. I see them in the woods. All the bodies, in the clothes they died in, their wounds fresh. Standing there, staring at me. They’re in my head, they’re in my soul.”

It’s all very King Lear. They embrace and it’s lovely.

Kevin goes to get the old man a brandy to calm his nerves. When he returns, the fear is gone from Conrad’s eyes. In its place: pure steel. And a shotgun pointed at Kevin’s head.

“If you breathe a word of this to Bella, I’ll kill you both,” he growls.

Conrad is another man again. As though the whole confession were just a performance. But it wasn’t. It was a glimpse of the terrifying disintegration of a man who can no longer distinguish between his different selves.

Kevin can’t believe it. “Pull the trigger then,” he shouts snottily.

“Fuck off now, Kevin,” Conrad says. And, like a beaten dog (though of course, Conrad would never treat a dog in this way), off Kevin fucks.

Well, we’ve spent most of this episode wondering about Frankie’s response to the murder of his son. But here we are. Harry calls Frankie. He wants to reach a treaty. He’s killed Frankie’s son, and now he hopes Frankie will back down.

Here we see a side to Frankie we haven’t seen before: crying, blubbering, a human man in real pain. He’s looking through old photographs of Michael.

“He’s dead, Harry,” he says through snot and tears. “Why did you have to do that? I can’t explain it. The pain.”

“It’s the game,” says Harry, heavy as stone.

To which Frankie replies: “Anyway, I’m sure you understand. After all, Harry, you’re a father too. Ain’tcha?”

It’s a less-than-veiled threat; Frankie’s message: this war isn’t over until the babies are all dead. He’s going after Gina.

And so we end the episode in truly miserable style. A montage of sadness, soundtracked by Johnny Cash’s cover of “Ain’t No Grave”:

The Harrigans have made a lot of enemies over the past eighteen episodes. And now, at last, the dead are joining the queue.

#6773. 13 Best Deodorant for Men 2026, According to Dermatologists

Frankly, the best deodorant for men sells itself. It stinks to stink: Body odor is right up there with bad breath as a totally avoidable misfortune. But while deodorant might seem like an odd category to get overly psyched about, as a longtime grooming editor, I can confidently say we're living in a golden age of the underarm essential.

Unlike the synthetic and chalky ghosts of deodorant sticks past, today’s best deodorants are a a sea of sophisticated formulas, improved textures, better scents, and naturally derived ingredients that, yes, actually work. The only hard part is honing in on the best one for you. Price, feel, skin sensitivity, and your sweat and odor levels matter. So I tested a whole lot of deodorants to narrow the field to seven standouts, some new, some tried-and-true, plus a few more deeper cuts I also loved.

I also tapped trusted dermatologists, for some expert insights further down. Just one last quick note: We’re not talking antiperspirants here, which primarily rely on aluminum compounds to reduce sweat. If that’s what you’re after, I’ve got recommendations for those, too.

Now, are there more targeted, more sophisticated, and, well, chicer deodorants on this list? Absolutely. But at the end of the day, you want your deo to do three things: keep the stink at bay, smell good, and be easy to repurchase. Dove Men+Care hits every mark, which is why it's in every drugstore.

The price, of course, is great, but then there's the scent: a surprisingly sophisticated blend of bright bergamot and warm white amber that's fresh, subtly citrusy, and softly warm. It smells so much better than your typical underarm fare and is actually a little mood-boosting, as the packaging suggests. The aluminum-free, baking-soda-free formula features moisture-attracting ingredients, giving it a little skincare credibility, too. It also feels super smooth going on, and kept its scent for most of the day. No notes!

Hip grooming brand Church California's deodorant only launched two years ago, but for me, it's everything a niche, forward-thinking deodorant should be: Effective, made with almost entirely naturally derived ingredients, and housed in compostable, plastic-free packaging. The formula uses magnesium and organic tapioca starch for odor and moisture control, with coconut oil and shea butter adding a little skin-conditioning goodness. And that scent? Wonderfully herbaceous, thanks to an essential oil blend that smells like the actual outdoors.

Admittedly, the sturdy cardboard tube takes a little getting used to—you push the stick up from the bottom instead of twisting a dial. And the glide is certainly a little less smooth. But oh, does the formula hold up. Even hours later, I'd catch little whiffs of those lovely botanicals, and when the scent eventually faded, there was still no detectable B.O. For the environmentally conscious guy, it's a no-brainer, and for the aesthete, it looks retro cool sitting on your medicine cabinet shelf.

Ursa Major’s Hoppin’ Fresh stick is a modern legend in the deo space. Among us grooming geeks, this au naturel-meets-elevated formula changed the underarm game when it launched in 2015. It was one of those early artisanal step-ups from drugstore fare, and it earned the hype with thoughtfully curated, foraged-sounding ingredients, a subtle, natural-smelling citrus scent, and a skincare-centric stick that glides on smoothly.

It still holds up today, fighting odor, soothing skin, and absorbing excess moisture to keep you smelling fresh. For an aluminum-free deo, it also keeps me pretty dry, too. And even the packaging—a sleek, solid-blue tube—feels like a style upgrade. Scent wise, in winter Forest Fix is the way to go. But the original Hoppin' Fresh is always waiting in the wings once the warmer months come around.

I'm well aware that Old Spice has plenty of newer, more fragrance-centric variations, many with names like Swagger. But I've always preferred the tried-and-true stuff, and that blue stick, here in High Endurance Refresh, is about as iconic as deodorant gets. It's my old reliable, and one I always keep as a backup, whether I'm visiting family, staying overnight somewhere, or just need something I can count on in a pinch. This classic has aged remarkably well, because it simply works.

Then there's the nostalgia, which hits me every time I catch a whiff of that distinctively fresh-meets-medicinal aroma I've loved unironically for years. The formula isn't particularly fancy: science-y ingredients give the aluminum-free stick its smooth glide, while glycerin adds a little skin-conditioning benefit. Would I wear this for an Ironman competition? Probably not. But for everyday errands, workdays, and just about everything in between? Absolutely. At under $15 for a three-pack, it's also easily one of the best value options on this list.

When you think gel deodorant, you probably picture those old-school perforated dispensers with tons of holes. But Baxter of California's is a smooth, transparent gel stick that's still, in my book, among the best. Part of the appeal is the formula, which packs some impressive botanicals: witch hazel and tea tree oil help tackle odor-causing bacteria, while chamomile helps soothe skin. Add tried-and-true glycerin to the mix, and you've got a gel deodorant with some serious skincare cred, too.

Then there's the application itself. The clear gel glides on effortlessly, with a cool, crisp, almost medicinal feel that's especially refreshing during the warmer months. Better still, it won't leave white marks on your shirts, something I always appreciate with my go-to black T-shirts. Is it the most heavy-duty odor fighter on this list? Not quite. But for everyday wear, it keeps the funk at bay without feeling like you've applied much of anything.

I test a lot of deodorants, but few become permanent fixtures in my bathroom. So it's worth telling you that Freaks of Nature's Everyday Endurance has become my primary deodorant lately. The brand may be known best for sunscreen, but it knows its way around our underarms too. And much like its sunscreens, part of the appeal is the application. The lotion-like formula emerges through a clever F-shaped grate, and there's something oddly satisfying about watching it dispense before smoothing it onto your underarms.

Better still, it absorbs well without any gunky, sticky residue. Cocoa butter and sunflower oil give it a skin-conditioning quality that makes this one of the most soothing deodorants I've ever worn. It's premium-priced, sure, but between the standout application, exemplary performance, and skin-conditioning feel, it's worth every penny.

Gone are the days when deodorant had to smell like “Sports Ice Rush.” Today's best odor fighters offer sophisticated, subtle scents (so they don't compete with your cologne), and Salt & Stone remains one of the top examples, with deodorants that have actual scent pyramids—Saffron & Cedar, Black Rose & Oud, Bergamot & Hinoki, for example. All are nuanced, refined, and never too loud.

But it's not just about the scent. The formula neutralizes odor and absorbs excess moisture very well, while skincare-centric additions like niacinamide and shea butter help keep underarms comfortable. More importantly, in my experience, the fragrance doesn't devolve into some funky olfactory murk by day's end. You still smell like the scent you applied that morning, or at the very least, fresh. Are these the heaviest-duty or most affordable deodorants out there? Nope. But they deliver on fragrance and function, and that's a combination worth investing in.

Caldera Lab just launched its first deodorant, and given the brand's skincare chops, it's no surprise the formula reads like a serum: niacinamide, aloe vera, and hyaluronic acid soothe and hydrate; postbiotics and magnesium tackle odor; and arrowroot absorbs moisture. Stacked, indeed.

Can Aesop do any wrong? Not really, and its deodorant spray is another example of the botanical-meets-aromatic goodness the amber-hued brand does so well. It's the only spray on this list, and while its odor protection holds up well, a midday refresh is sometimes warranted—and honestly, part of the pleasure.

If your skin is extra sensitive, Nécessaire's entire fragrance-free lineup is exemplary, and its deodorant stick is no exception. It keeps odor impressively in check, is easy on sensitive underarms, and won't compete with your favorite cologne. It's also aluminum- and baking soda-free, dermatologist-tested, hypoallergenic, and even carries the National Eczema Association's Seal of Acceptance.

Cremo may be kings of shave cream, but it knows a thing or two about deodorant, too. The formulas are affordable, the odor protection holds up, and the scents smell so damn good. Take Palo Santo, with its warm, woody, slightly smoky aroma that feels far more sophisticated than its price suggests. Coconut oil and shea butter add the skin-conditioning touch you'd expect from a pricier stick. rant quietly in the background, look elsewhere—but if you like a lot of fragrance with your funk-fighting, Cremo delivers serious bang for the buck.

The Grey's culty, rightfully hyped deodorant is the best old-school-style roll-on I've ever tried. The unscented formula is so lightweight you almost have to check you're applying anything, but oh, you are, and there's a cooling tingle as it goes on. It's the most expensive deodorant on this list, but with its premium slim aluminum casing and impressively lightweight formula, it's an investment in barely-there odor-fighting care.

Malin+Goetz's Eucalyptus Deodorant is a stone-cold classic that helped elevate gel sticks and make them feel refreshingly natural. The scent is practically a trademark at this point, equal parts medicinal, herbal, and oh-so-subtle. But there's substance behind it: eucalyptus extract helps refresh skin, while odor-neutralizing citronellyl methylcrotonate keeps the funk at bay.

As a grooming editor, I've tried more sticks, sprays, gels, and roll-ons than I can count, from drugstore staples to niche newcomers. And while plenty have impressed me on first swipe, the real test is whether I keep reaching for them weeks, months, or even years later.

Here, I drew on firsthand testing, focusing on odor protection, scent, texture, application, skin comfort, and, of course, how well they hold up. I've worn these deodorants through warm and cool weather, long days, and everything in between. Some are favorites I've relied on for years, while others are newer that are now part of my medicine cabinet rotation. As I've said, the best deodorant sometimes depends on the occasion or even the season, so it's a good idea to have a few on hand if possible. At the end of the day, a deodorant needs to keep B.O. at bay, ideally absorb excess moisture, and feel comfortable. Some excel in certain areas more than others, but each one above warrants its spot among the best deodorants.

Here is some dermatologist advice on deodorants—the best ingredients to look for, plus some controversial ones, too—with insights from board-certified dermatologists Kiran Mian, Ryan B. Turner, and Heather Rogers.

The primary cause of body odor is “bad” bacteria pairing up with sweat. There are two ways to prevent the stink: Reduce perspiration in the area, and/or create an inhospitable environment for the bacteria.

These are the most common ingredients to seek out if you want to temper the proliferation of bacteria in your pits (and anywhere you’re particularly pungent).

This antimicrobial and anti-inflammatory mineral safely and effectively kills bacteria without irritating skin, says Mian.

Dr. Mian also endorses the non-irritating magnesium hydroxide, a naturally occurring compound that neutralizes bacteria without absorbing into the skin.

“The terpenes in tea tree oil make this ingredient naturally antibacterial and antifungal, helping to reduce body odor,” Turner says. “In its pure, concentrated form, it can be very irritating. It’s best to use a product that’s formulated with tea tree oil at low percentages. It’s still effective when diluted, and a little bit goes a very long way.”

This one is controversial to some. Most skin types will tolerate baking soda (sodium bicarbonate) just fine, but only trust reputable, well-reviewed brands who use it. “Baking soda has a very alkaline pH level, while that of the skin is more acidic,” explains Turner. “This disrupts the skin’s natural barrier, harming the good bacteria in our microbiome, and leaving our skin more susceptible to irritation and inflammation.” (We’ve included a few deodorants with baking soda below, plus many natural picks that explicitly exclude it.)

It’s easy to get these two confused, but they share a principle. “Prebiotics feed the good bacteria on our skin (bacteria that does not break down sweat releasing a foils odor), which then overpopulates those odor-producing bacteria,” explains Mian. “Probiotics are the good bacteria that will ideally populate the skin and overtake odor causing bacteria.” The names of these might be strange to you, like lactobacillus acidophilus or saccharomyces ferment. Chances are, a brand will proudly tout their pre- and probiotics, so look for that indication instead.

“These chemical exfoliating ingredients help resurface the skin, which also strips away bacteria as well as removes the dead skin that bacteria break down, which causes body odor,” says Turner. And Dr. Rogers echoes the love: “Using acids to exfoliate the skin in our armpits helps rebuild the acidic mantle [which is compromised with age], leading to better control of bacterial growth and odor. Many acids work great; glycolic, lactic acid and mandelic acid are my favorites. Salicylic acid, a beta hydroxy acid, has a higher chance of irritation.”

“Activated charcoal can help to absorb sweat and help to neutralize odors,” says Turner.

Arrowroot powder, tapioca starch, and cornstarch are popular ingredients in aluminum-free deodorants, helping absorb excess moisture and keep underarms feeling dry without blocking sweat production. Of tapioca starch, Mian says, “This non-irritating starch won’t clog sweat ducts like aluminum, which can cause breakouts.” (It's also terrific in anti-chafe products for similar moisture-absorbing benefits.) “Like tapioca starch, corn starch pulls moisture from the skin to leave a powdery feeling behind. Unless you have an allergy to these plants, they are safer to use,” says Turner. Both doctors particularly favor arrowroot powder as a naturally derived, generally well-tolerated ingredient for absorbing moisture.

Both help soak up surface moisture without blocking sweat production. Freaks of Nature's formula includes bentonite clay.

A naturally occurring, porous mineral powder that absorbs excess moisture and helps keep underarms dry. You'll find it in Ursa Major's deodorant.

Approach your deodorant purchase with the following considerations:

While it’s easy to celebrate a fully natural formula—and for good reason—keep in mind that not all natural ingredients are inherently good for your skin. Ingredients like baking soda and certain essential oils can cause irritation, particularly if your underarms are sensitive. Conversely, synthetic ingredients aren't automatically bad for your skin, either. “What’s more important is to consider the manufacturer of the products—are they reputable? Do they perform extensive testing? What are the ingredients used and from where are they sourced?” says Turner. That's sound advice whether you're shopping natural or synthetic. There's a reason dermatologists frequently recommend established brands like Dove: They have the resources to develop, test, and manufacture formulas at scale, often at a fraction of the price of more niche alternatives. Of course, that doesn't mean smaller brands aren't doing their homework, either. Finding the right deodorant still takes some trial and error, gents, but you can always start with our list.

If your underarms are easily irritated, start with the ingredient list. Baking soda, strong fragrances, essential oils, and certain acids can trigger irritation, though everyone's skin reacts differently. Look for skin-conditioning ingredients like shea butter, glycerin, and aloe, several of which you'll find in the deodorants featured above. Fragrance-free formulas are a smart starting point for sensitive skin, while unscented are worth considering, too—just know that some contain masking fragrances.

Deodorants come in all sorts of formats these days, from classic solid sticks and clear gels to sprays, roll-ons, lotions, and pastes. How they feel when you apply them matters as much as how well they work. Some glide on more smoothly, while others feel chalkier, thicker, or more moisturizing. Gels and roll-ons tend to feel lightweight and refreshing (great for warmer weather), while richer lotions and pastes feel more nourishing and suit the cooler months. Ultimately, the best deodorant feels comfortable, doesn't leave residue on your clothes, and fits your lifestyle.

Dr. Kiran Mian is a board-certified dermatologist at Hudson Dermatology and Laser Surgery in NYC, and volunteer faculty at the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai University.

Dr. Ryan B. Turner is a board-certified dermatologist and founder of Turner Dermatology in NYC and the TRNR Skin skin care line.

Dr. Heather Rogers is a board-certified dermatologist and co-founder of Modern Dermatology in Seattle and the founder of the Doctor Rogers skin care line.

#6770. The Black Doves Season 2 Finale Has an Insane Cliffhanger

Let’s talk about Helen Webb and what it all means for season 3.

After a two-year wait, Netflix's Christmas crime caper Black Doves is finally back for its second outing. It's a messy delight that tests the loyalties of the central characters in ways the first season never dared and—both annoyingly and excitingly—ends on just as agonizing a cliffhanger.

In the first season, Elizabeth Reed (Sarah Lancashire) was something of a mystery: an ominous, witty controller of sorts who kept Helen Webb (Keira Knightley) and Sam Young (Ben Whishaw) on their toes. But this time around, Black Doves spends some time unravelling her past, as well as that of the titular intelligence agency: a backstory that informs and explains the major threat of the season, the German anarchist organization Kestrel.

Though the secrets reveal themselves slowly, a bombshell-filled (pun very much intended) finale crashes through a series of twists that—if you didn't see them coming (which I can't say I did, for some of them)—will really feel like a stab in the face. No, not the back, because unfortunately the answers were always lying right in front of us.

There's a lot to break down from that last episode, so read on for our recap of Black Doves season 2 and an explanation of the ending—including that twist that'll probably have a drastic impact on where we'll be at the beginning of season 3.

The penultimate episode ended with a bunch of crossed wires, conflicting contracts, and potential deaths. Eleanor and Williams have taken out paid hits on Laila and Sam, while Sam's final target in his prison-break contract with Kestrel is none other than Reed (after episode 4 revealed that she is in fact the mysterious “Wildflower” of the files she tried to take from Alexi Chernov).

The main action kicks off when the precious international peace summit that Wallace (Andrew Buchan) has spent the whole season planning gets upended before it even starts. Wallace and Helen's cab is intercepted and chased, while U. S. defense envoy Cecile Mason (Neve Campbell) and recently widowed Russian envoy Katia Chernov (Sylvia Hoeks) are captured by strange armed men.

When Patrick (Sam Riley), the Kestrel man who's been blackmailing Sam all season, escorts Reed back into the Black Doves office with a gun to her head, it quickly becomes clear that 'Wildflower', or Reed, is the only target Kestrel wants brought in alive. Or, at least, whoever's running it does.

And there's the twist: after a horrid little hostage situation in the office results in the deaths of quite a few senior Black Doves and Mason, Katia reveals that she is in fact the head of Kestrel and, in turn, Vita—the long-lost, abandoned daughter that Reed left in Berlin after killing her fake husband and running off (ouch).

Dani, the nuisance of a secretary Reed planted in Wallace's office to seduce him and potentially replace Helen, has ended up being recruited by Kestrel. But she doesn't last long after a game of grenade hot potato played by Eleanor, Williams, and Laila ends with Dani being caught in a lift with it. Even Reed's capable of mistakes.

Meanwhile, over in the car park where Helen and Wallace end up after fleeing, Mr. Prime Minister finally decides to question his wife about her past and her secrets. Obviously, she lies again—until the situation shifts a little.

Helen receives a series of ominous messages and threatening instructions, first over text, then through a discreet earpiece planted in her car. Katia, whom Helen very quickly realizes is not an ally, instructs her to kill Wallace and turn herself in to MI5. But what's the leverage, you ask?

Marie, the housekeeper who was teased as a Black Dove at the end of the first season, has in fact been working for Kestrel this whole time. If you're a babysitter to the Prime Minister's spy wife, who's been planted by a capitalist intelligence agency, but you're working for a German anarchist organization, does that make you a double or a triple agent? A nightmare for Mrs Webb, more than anything.

Helen tricks Katia over the phone by shooting past Wallace towards the window, explains (on a surface level) that the children are in danger and she needs to hijack the car, and darts off with Sam to rescue the children from Marie.

After confronting Marie, Helen shoots her in the head and flees the scene, while Reed reconciles (not quite) with her bleeding-out daughter in her last moments. Kestrel's down and all is well? Obviously not, because it's never that simple.

MI5 is even more suspicious of Helen than ever before, but Wallace is covering for her. Conteh, as one of the few surviving senior members of the Black Doves, restores Reed to her former standing.

Helen and Sam reconcile properly after their heated argument during the first Reed rescue mission of the season, and he reveals that he's going to leave the country once again. After they part, a strange man bumps into Helen and, BOOM, final twist! Patrick has escaped in perfect condition and Kestrel is still going strong. He leaves Helen with his card, more as a threat than anything else.

Reed and Helen agree to increase Helen's standing and role within the Black Doves, meaning that she'll be more than just an intel-grabber going forward, and putting her in a pretty good spot work-wise. However, things aren't quite so simple at home.

Wallace confronts Helen once again about her real name (Lily Morris), and the finale ends with what appears to be the beginning of a conversation about her past. But how much is Helen actually going to tell Wallace? There's only so much she can give away without completely compromising her position, but she'll have to say enough to regain his trust. We'll find out in two years, I suppose.

#6769. Bob Dylan Dressed Like Timothée Chalamet to Meet the Pope

Today, millions of Americans awoke to social media feeds awash with images of and commentary about a landmark event that seemed nigh-impossible a mere 24 hours earlier. I speak, of course, of this image:

As the nation began to digest the results of a historic election that seemed to herald the arrival of a new America, two of our very best Americans met for a private audience on the other side of the Atlantic: Bob Dylan, the Great American artist, and Pope Leo XIV, the first American Pope. Dylan is in the midst of yet another lengthy European tour—his strongest in some number of years, judging by recent bootleg tapes. On November 2, he played a show at Milan’s Unipol Dome, then jetted over to Rome ahead of a November 5 performance at the ​​Palazzo dello Sport. What better way to kill an off-day than a quick chat with the Vicar of Christ?

It wasn’t the first time Bob met a Pope, as some pointed out. In 1997, he performed for John Paul II at the World Eucharistic Congress in Bologna, donning a dapper Western suit and a fine Stetson for the occasion. He and JP2 made for a bit of an odd couple, the mysterious American gunslinger and the seemingly-nonplussed Polish holy man.

Dylan has somewhat more in common with Pope Leo, a.k.a Robert Francis Prevost, a fellow Midwesterner. But even if the two men hail from similar geography, the sight of them together under the bright fluorescent lights of an anonymous Vatican office looked like the product of a well-crafted AI prompt, if not an outright fever dream. The images were so unexpected, so uncanny, I would’ve been convinced they were fake had they not been published by an official press organ of the Vatican.

Much of the whiplash stemmed from the two men’s appearances. Angel and devil. Spy versus spy. The man in the long black coat and the man in the short white mozzetta. The one liners wrote themselves.

On Wednesday, the Pope wore a clean white cassock and a tidy little zucchetto, with an iron cross hanging down around his neck. In short, he looked like the Pope. I suppose I would say the same about Bob: that he looked like himself. As any good Dylan fan knows, however, the meaning of that phrase has changed radically over the years. Bob’s shifting appearance is almost as notorious as his shifting artistic sensibility. Dust bowl refugee, sharp-suited mod, bohemian poltergeist, whatever this is—the man has worn many hats.

A hoodied Timothée Chalamet, seen with Kylie Jenner, in New York City.

Sometime in the early 1990s, Dylan took a liking to the humble hoodie, a stylistic evolution that intersected neatly with his booze-fueled artistic nadir. The hoodie allowed him to hide in plain sight, even as he continued to take the stage around the world on his Never Ending Tour. It was practical, yes, but it was also a defense mechanism, a way to synthesize his compulsion to perform and his aversion to being seen. That it often looked hard as hell did not hurt. (It should come as little surprise that Timothée Chalamet, who may as well have been born wearing a hoodie, seemed to riff on this era when he was channeling Bob all those months ago.)

In the 21st century, Bob came unstuck from time. On landmark records like “Love And Theft” and Modern Times, Dylan burrowed into an imaginary musical past situated somewhere between the Civil War and the Great Depression. His style shifted to match. In the early aughts, he adopted a full country-western guise, complete with a Lee Van Cleef mustache. In the years that followed, his sartorial inspirations shifted east: a little less cowboy, a little more riverboat gambler, often performing in wide-brimmed hats, long frock coats, and string ties. “Rock and roll Mark Twain” wouldn’t have been an inaccurate descriptor.

Which brings us back to today’s meeting with Pope Leo. On first glance, Dylan’s ensemble is bonkers: western black blouson with contrast stitching along the chest and collar; beneath that, a heavyweight black hoodie zipped to the chest; beneath that, a white collared shirt with several buttons undone, collar loosely drawn together with a polka-dot cravat. The hood is decidedly up, stationed atop a black flat brim cap. In some shots, Bob can even be seen sporting leather gloves and an old-fashioned umbrella, which seems to double as a cane.

Part “Man in Black” sobriety, part rock star debauchery, with a dash of the hypebeast flavor from Timmy’s A Complete Unknown press tour—the man got a damn fit off in Vatican City. There’s no other way to describe it. (It’s a shame Pope Leo left his Rhude jacket back at the Papal Palace.)

Still, it’s a look with roots in Bob’s past, or pasts: the hoodies of the ’90s, the Westernwear of the 2000s, even the polka dots of 1965. No one but Bob would ever dare to pair all these disparate elements, particularly for a private audience with the Bishop of Rome. No one but Bob could.

Someone should come up with a word for that.

#6768. The Chicest Fall and Winter Coat Trends to Inspire Your Cold-Weather Wardrobe

Fall is the official start of coat season, and honestly, it might just be the best time of year to have fun with your wardrobe. The right coat can transform even the simplest outfit into a seriously stylish moment, making it the ultimate investment piece for your fall rotation. Whether you're dressing for a coffee run, a weekend brunch, or a night out, there's a perfect outerwear look waiting to inspire you.

The best part about a good coat is that you don't need an entirely new wardrobe to still bring in the compliments. Just find a coat you love, build your outfit around it, and let your outerwear do all the talking. Consider this your sign to make getting dressed the most exciting part of your day. If you need some inspiration, I’ve got you covered with some different styles you can tap into.

Cropped trench coats are the ultimate fall layering hack. Throw one over a fitted knit and wide-leg jeans for an effortlessly chic, waist-defining look. With all the timeless polish of a classic trench but a fresh, fashion-forward twist, this playful silhouette is the perfect way to give your fall wardrobe a little main-character energy.

A patterned coat is the easiest way to make a statement this fall. Style it over a simple monochromatic outfit to let the print do all the talking. From bold checks to playful plaids, this cozy layer brings personality, texture, and a little unexpected fun to even the most everyday look.

Make a bold entrance this fall in a colorful faux fur coat, pairing it with sleek denim, a simple sweater, and boots to let the statement-making texture shine. It's cozy, a little bit glam, and guaranteed to brighten up even the gloomiest fall day. Especially something like this burgundy coat, a popular color that’s been trending for a while.

Cinched-waist chic is having a moment, and a belted coat is the perfect way to define your silhouette while keeping your fall layers looking polished. It’s also my preferred everyday coat for the cooler days, keeping you fully wrapped and warm.

A good puffer jacket will take you far. Pair it with a miniskirt and chunky boots, or dress it down with a baseball cap and some jeans. Doesn’t matter the styling, it’s a great everyday coat to have on deck.

Aiyana Ishmael is the style editor at Cosmopolitan magazine. In her work, Aiyana focuses on the culture of fashion and how it intertwines and shapes the zeitgeist. She is an award-winning journalist from Miami, Florida, and a graduate of the historically Black university, Florida A&M. She is a 2024 Forbes 30 Under 30: Media honoree, and her debut romance novella PASSING GAME is set to release March of 2027 (831 Stories/Simon & Schuster).

#6765. Matt Damon on the Oscars, Tarantino, and the Roles That Got Away

In the desert, as the song goes, you can remember your name. So it’s fitting that Matt Damon has come here—to a sweltering stretch of inland California near Lancaster—to reflect on his 30-odd-year career as one of the most familiar and bankable faces in Hollywood, and specifically on a year that will go down as one of that career’s most challenging and rewarding passages.

Damon set 2026 off with The Rip, a throwback pulp thriller that reteamed him with his lifetime BFF Ben Affleck. He then went on to dominate the summer to a degree most actors crossing the other side of 55 wouldn’t dream of, with Christopher Nolan’s The Odyssey—a noisy, thrilling, engrossing feat of big-budget moviemaking that wouldn’t have worked without an Odysseus compelling enough to command audiences to take the 20-year journey with him. The role demanded everything in Damon’s toolbox, and then some, but the result was a cultural and commercial run that only Spider-Man himself could top.

Jacket (sold as suit) by Norton & Sons. Shirt by Dolce & Gabbana. Pants by Celine. Cummerbund by Charvet. Pocket square by Budd Shirtmakers. Boots by Tom Ford.

This is rare air, and Damon knows it. Lightning struck once for him, with Good Will Hunting in 1997—he never thought he’d see that happen again, much less 29 years later. If exhaustion is the price of beating those odds, then so be it. Still readjusting to life on dry land, Damon sat down to unpack the most unlikely filmmaking experience of his career—and begin working out where he might want to go next.

This interview has been condensed and edited.

Matt Damon: That is amazing coming from him. Yes, I did hear about it.

Yeah, I think if you talk to any actor my age, any of the usual suspects, they’d probably say the same thing. I think the more you do it, if you’re working with really good people, you’re always improving, you’re getting better. And then you’re living more life and you have your family dynamics, all of your primary relationships kind of fill up your reservoir. I’ve talked to other actors my age about it, Ben included—I think there’s a tendency to push when you’re younger, a little bit, to try to work yourself up, and the older you get, the more available a lot of this stuff is. These very, very, very big emotions are kind of just sitting [near the surface]. And so you can really relax into the work, and it can be very full without being forced.

I think so, yeah. I mean, I’ve never been a part of a movie like The Odyssey that was…. Well, I guess Oppenheimer, to an extent, was somewhat of a phenomenon as well.

My workload was a lot heavier on this one, and everybody’s was, and that was kind of the joy of it. We knew we were the first ones to ever try to do a full film in IMAX [70 mm], and that comes with a whole host of challenges for every single department. We were all, I think, very patient with each other because we knew what was being asked of the other people. So in terms of the acting department—the IMAX load is two and a half minutes or three minutes. And so these big, heavy emotional scenes—you couldn’t play them all the way through. You’d have to cut them up. And the crew was just awesome, very patient with us. It felt like we were explorers, in a way. We got to be the first ones to do it, and that really bonded us.

I never would have expected it. And also, to play a role that’s so physical and, at my age, to even have a role that’s available on a scale like this. I’d always wanted to make a David Lean movie. To have it come at this point in my life, I felt incredibly lucky, not only to be able to do it, but also to be able to contextualize it. I don’t think I would’ve appreciated it as much 20 years ago.

No, I just don’t think I would’ve been able to understand and to hold as much gratitude as I am and as I did throughout the process. It’s all about how you look at things. And in retrospect, yeah, we were probably cold and wet. There was a lot of physical discomfort that was temporary. But because I felt so grateful to be there, I was just so excited. I was just so joyful the whole time that I didn’t really notice any of that.

[Laughs] There are no sure things, man. I think that’s where you get into trouble, being like, Well, this is a surefire hit. I’ve heard that before.

I felt it when I saw it. There is that thing that can happen where you make a movie, and you feel like it’s really good, and it just doesn’t connect, and there’s just nothing you can do about that. But as the audience reactions started to come in and the critical reactions were great as well, it was like, Okay….

Chris [Nolan], the most nervous he is in the entire process, from the very beginning to the very end, is the eve of the film coming out. He’s genuinely nervous. He talks openly about it, because that is the moment that you put it out into the world and you have to live with the reaction. Ultimately, the audience decides. Once it opened and the responses were what they were, I think I felt like, We’re going to be okay. It was a big bet in the sense that it was a big budget. I’m not used to playing in that arena. The numbers that movies have to get to just to break even at that level are just so beyond the movies that I’m known for.

A few of them. But The Martian, which I think is the biggest movie that I’ve ever headlined—I think The Odyssey made three times what that made. And that was a real hit for me. But there’s this other league of these kinds of movies that are in the billions that I’ve just never….

Well, the thing about a role like that is every single day I was paired with an unbelievable scene partner. One day I’d be working with Annie [Hathaway], and then the next day I’d be working with Himesh [Patel]. It’s following this character through all these different stages of his life and people who are close to him. So every day was a different, exciting little challenge, a little puzzle to figure out. I’ve always said that when you’re working with a great actor, they’re so good that they’re good enough for both of you. And it’s almost like this wave comes and you just stand up on your board and ride it. Then you have Samantha Morton, who literally just picks you up and shoulder-carries you.

It’s very hard to come in and do really intense things in a very short time frame. [Morton’s scenes]—that was under a week of shooting. Each day being incredibly intense. And that was what was so incredible about these actors who would come in. They’re under a lot of pressure. Nobody says that, but we all know the deal. These are critical scenes, and everybody just came in and killed it. It just got more and more exciting as we were going.

I agree. She’s incredible. She’s always been incredible. We had a near-miss working together in 2003—and I got to know her then—in a movie with Heath Ledger. I just think the world of her as an actress, and I was excited when we did the scene. I was like, This one’s going to really work. And I hope—it doesn’t always happen right away, but hopefully it’s lodged her back in the minds of people who are making those decisions, because she’s just extraordinary.

That’s the emotional spine of the movie. A lot of that monologue that I have at the end is in voiceover, but I wrote it all out and I had it on my phone and I would just carry it with me off set. I would just revisit it and run through it a few times, and I was doing that for months and months. Firstly, I thought it was just so beautifully written, but also because I knew that scene had to land. If that lands, then the movie works.

And that also means setting them up as a young couple before he leaves. There’s a lot of engineering that goes into getting to a scene like that and then you have to execute it. I spent an inordinate amount of time thinking about that one.

Well, at that point, the audience has been on the journey with you, and it’s been a hell of a journey, so you don’t have to tell them that. We were tired. That scene I shot with Samantha, oddly, we had come from Iceland, but we were shooting nights, and we came to Scotland, and I had it happen two or three times on this movie where I just didn’t sleep. I was tired and I went to bed, and I’m sure everyone’s had that feeling where you’ve got to get up the next day and you have something that you have to do and you can’t fall asleep and you’re kicking the blanket around and you’re staring at the ceiling. By the time I got to work to do the scene with Sam, I hadn’t slept in 24 hours, and by the end of the day it was 36 or whatever. I remember thinking the great thing about this character is he probably didn’t sleep last night. Don’t worry about it. So the weariness was very real for a lot of the movie.

Coat and pants by Dries Van Noten. Shirt by Dior. Cummerbund (throughout) by Charvet.

It’s all in there because it’s what I’m actually feeling. As I was saying before, it’s very easy to relax into what’s real and available and to have access to it.

This was the biggest one that we’ve ever done, and we’ve always had a two-week rule. I think the first stretch [on The Odyssey], I was away for three and a half weeks. The first half of the movie was the hardest because the kids were still in school. And then once the summer hit, they could come to set and we could be together. But that first stretch—luckily they’re huge Chris Nolan fans and they knew that it was this incredible role, so they were really supportive, and it was a sacrifice for them, and they were awesome about it.

He’s turned down a lot of work. And in fact, he really wanted to take on the CEO role at [Damon and Affleck’s production company] Artists Equity because it would keep him home. So when he directs—for instance, Animals, that was all shot in LA. He said to me when we started the company—he’s got joint custody, which means he gets to be with the kids half the time. And he said, “If I go to Estonia and do a movie, it’s not like I get that time back.” So that for him means he’s got to stay in LA.

I felt that way when I did Air with Ben. It was such a joyful experience, and we had so much fun, and we really loved the movie we made. We had this discussion about how I don’t want to do [a project] unless it’s joyful and unless it’s really right for my life. So much of being a young actor is you’re afraid it’s going to go away. You’re very much still in touch with that feeling of being unemployed. And so when Ben and I got famous, we both really worked. Buried ourselves in work. Partly because people are suddenly offering you things and you can’t imagine turning anything down, right? It just seems counter to everything you’ve been trying so hard to do for your entire life.

The first time was before The Bourne Identity came out. I had done The Legend of Bagger Vance with Will [Smith] and Charlize [Theron], and that movie didn’t do well. And Will was, as he is now, the biggest star. And so it’s like, it’s not Will’s fault. [Laughs] And then I did All the Pretty Horses with Billy Bob [Thornton], and that was a whole fraught experience. There were different ideas between the studio and between Billy Bob about what the movie should be, and so it ended up getting cut in a way that wasn’t to Billy’s liking and it didn’t do well. And so I had had these misses. And so there was this stretch where the phone just didn’t ring. The silence was…. It was a real rose-colored lenses coming off.

Well, the other thing was that The Bourne Identity was delayed something like a year. And so the word was, That’s going to be a stinker. But in that time, I went right from [filming] Bourne to Ocean’s Eleven. Then there was this whole period of time where nobody was calling, except for Steven Soderbergh, who called and offered me the movie The Informant! And I was so confused. I asked him, “Are you asking me to write it?” Because I couldn’t imagine he was asking me to be in it, because nobody else was calling. He goes, “What? No, Scott Burns is writing it. I want you to be the lead.” And I’ve never forgotten that. He just paid no attention to what everyone else was saying. But anyway, leaving that aside, nobody else was calling. And then The Bourne Identity came out, and it was a big hit.

It’s not personal. It’s business. And people can’t really go out of their way to help you. It’s really a pretty cold calculus that determines whether or not you’re on the list or off the list. And you never know who’s on the list. And the list can be different for different studios, who they’ll greenlight a movie with.

No, no. There’s very, very frank conversations between agents and studio people going like, “I can’t greenlight the movie with X, but I can with Y.” And that list is always changing, and I know that sometimes I’m on it and sometimes I’m not. I just have to remember it’s not personal, but you can feel it if you’re on the list or not.

Yeah. And [the] quality of projects [you’re offered]. I’m sure I’ve worked my way off that list a number of times. I’ve had some movies that didn’t work, and then you got to pay attention. I remember 2012 to 2014-ish, maybe, Patrick [Whitesell], my agent for 35 years, said I was taking a lot of supporting roles—because again, I’m not strategizing at all. I’m just like, Oh, True Grit. I want to work with the Coen brothers. Great.

Yup, George [Clooney directing], great. So there was this string of movies like that. Patrick called me and he said, “You’ll become a supporting actor. We got to think about this next one.” And then what happened to come along was The Martian. But that’s about as much strategizing as we’ve ever done.

Interstellar was in there too. I’m so happy that I’m in that movie. I love the role, even though it’s a tiny part. When Chris offered me [the role], we hadn’t worked together, and [when] he offered me the part in Interstellar, he said, “Do you know that saying ‘There are no small parts, there are only small actors’ ? This is a small part.” But I think he undersold it, because it’s a really kind of pivotal role, and I had a ball doing it.

Yeah, I think I was on a Bourne movie or about to go do Green Zone with Paul Greengrass. That and Milk. I was supposed to play the Dan White part. And I was really excited about it. I’d spoken to Sean [Penn], and I’d started the research. I’d been watching the documentaries about him. Then Sean’s schedule changed and the movie had to push three months, which meant that it was going to conflict with the movie I was going to do with Paul. So I had to bow out, and I just loved that script so much. And I love Gus [Van Sant], obviously. And so that was a bummer. But having said that, they got Josh Brolin, and Josh is absolutely brilliant in the movie. That helped a lot, I think, with this feeling that I have, that I do believe that the right actor gets the part.

I never like to say that, because I don’t want to live with the regret, but I can tell you that I passed on Ted, and I’ve never had this experience where I laughed so hard reading a script. I literally took it to dinner and was reading parts of it out to my friends at dinner. And then I just got too clever by half. I don’t know what I was thinking. I was like, Well, maybe the teddy bear’s actually the best part.…

So I overthought it and I talked myself out of it. And because it shot in Boston, it would’ve been a chance for me to be home—my father’s passed away now, and I look back and I go, Man, that would’ve been six months back [home].

But, again, the right actor got the part. Mark [Wahlberg’s] great, and the movie’s great, and I wouldn’t have made it any better.

They’re really hard to do. And I don’t read a lot [of them]. I don’t think people think of me for comedies a lot. But I love them, and I love watching them, and I love doing them if I can get my hands on one. But they’re few and far between. I think because they don’t travel well. They’re harder to get made because you really have to get all your money back in America. Comedy is so cultural. So the margin for error, I think, is smaller, which is what’s so great when you see a great one.

Anthony [Minghella], I think, was the first, with Talented Mr. Ripley. And he talked openly about it. A lot of these directors do. They want to use whatever it is that you have to offer in a new way and in a fun way and in an unexpected way. Some of those roles are the best roles that I’ve ever had a chance to play. Really interesting, complex people, and I love that.

I shot a scene in a movie theater with [Jack Nicholson], and Jack was working with an earwig. It was just the three of us before the crew came in, and we were just rehearsing. Marty [Scorsese] goes, “Let’s run this and see how we think it’s going to go.” And Jack took the earpiece out and we rehearsed the scene a few times and Jack was letter-perfect with his dialogue. And so Marty went to get the crew, and as people were coming in I said, “Hey, man, what’s the deal with the earwig? You obviously know all your lines—you’re writing stuff.” And it was really interesting because, he goes, “The older you get, the harder it is to remember your lines.” And that feeling of going up on a line, that panic, that surge of adrenaline that you get—he goes, “It just makes me so uncomfortable.” It’s a fail-safe. And if he knows [the earpiece is] there, his body stays in that relaxed state. People might think it was laziness. It was the opposite. He was a grinder. He worked really, really hard. It was more about just protecting himself.

No, no, no, no, no. I think everybody was really focused on Marty. And I remember I was shooting second unit for The Bourne Ultimatum during that Oscars, so I didn’t go. I was living in Miami at the time, and I had an infant. [My wife] Lucy and I were laying in bed watching it and so nervous for Marty. And then when he won, I was jumping up and down in the bedroom and I remember being really excited. And then the next category was best picture, and The Departed won. And I realized in that moment I hadn’t even considered that that could happen. And I turned to Lucy and I was like, “Shit, we should be there!” And then the next few weeks, I got all these calls from all the cast and the studio, like, “Hey, where were you, man?” I totally blew it. And then the next time that that happened was on Oppenheimer, and we had this whole scary thing happen where my wife had a brain aneurysm.

She’s totally fine, thank God, but [she] had this brain surgery the week before the Oscars. But it was the first night she felt good enough to get up and had her energy back. And she surprised me and got a dress and we went out to the after-party, because she remembered that the last time it happened, I didn’t do it. And she was like, “This time we’re going out.” And it was pretty fucking heroic of her to do that and really meaningful.

It’s not something you can control. You got to just do the work, and that’s got to be the reward. Because it’s like, then if you don’t win, do you feel bad about something that was really positive? Don’t turn this into something bad. It’s a tricky one. I always think about old movies when I look at them and it says, “So-and-so won an Oscar, so-and-so didn’t.” You’re judging it in a totally different way. It’s not attached to that anymore. I think the real way they should do them, really, is 20 or 25 years later. You’ll have a really good idea of which movies really endured and really still matter to you. There can be hype in the moment, or it can be a zeitgeist thing, but it doesn’t hold up. The goal for me is to make as many of those movies that are around 20 years later. That’s a cool thing. People come up [to me] about Good Will Hunting and I realize they weren’t born when the movie came out. It’s just very cool for Ben and me to have a new generation of people actually see it.

I don’t know what that means. I live in Brooklyn. [Laughs] If the trail is in that area, yeah. I don’t know what that entails. I will say, the technical side of this movie—I think of someone like Willie Burton, our sound guy, who’s in his 80s and he is a legend in the business.

And I did not loop a line in this movie, which is incredible. And I did not loop a line in Oppenheimer, which is incredible. Willie and Doug [Shamburger], his boom operator, are so good. Every scene on the ship, Willie is down below in the hold mixing in storms. The fact that he salvages all of that dialogue is nothing short of amazing. The technical achievement of our heads of department and their teams were really special. And I just hope that people realize that, realize what it took to do what they did, because from a technical standpoint, I hope they all get their flowers.

I didn’t think I was going to work. And then the Daniels called me and they had this project that was so out there, and it was such a Daniels movie and it’s so different from anything I’ve ever been able to do that I couldn’t pass it up. They’re really good people, and this movie’s like a cosmic love bomb for the world. So it felt like the right place to put my energy, but we’re almost done shooting it, and yeah, I’m going to take a long time off.

I haven’t thought about it. There are a lot of great performances. Paul Newman had some great ones at the end. We have the studio now with Artists Equity, and I feel like we’re just going to do stuff we really want to do, because it’s a very time-consuming job, and if you’re not 100 percent in, it’s just not worth doing. You have to really let it take over your life. I think that was one of the joys of The Odyssey—it dominated our lives.

There was a funny thing that happened on The Odyssey. We were in Scotland. I was with my kids. They’d just come over and gotten out of school, and we were eating dinner in this tiny hotel in this tiny town. And so there was one little dining room, which had 10 little tables in it, and the crew had watched dailies and so they were shuffling in, and I was already eating with my kids—I had jeans and a flannel shirt and a baseball hat on. And Chris came in and he walked by the table and I said, “Hey, Chris.” And he turned and he goes, “Oh, hey, Matt. I didn’t recognize you with the beard.” Chris doesn’t say stupid things—it was definitely the stupidest thing he’s ever said, because we’d been next to each other for six months.

He had just walked out of dailies watching close-ups of me, I’m sure with this giant beard. And I looked at him and he immediately realized what he’d said and he just looked at his shoes and then he looked up, like, Can we just agree that this never happened? And he shuffled off, and my kids go, “Dad, does Chris Nolan not know who you are?” I was like, “No, guys, he’s just exhausted.” He was that tired. He hadn’t seen me out of wardrobe in a while, you know what I mean? We were so tired by the end of that movie.

Well, he should write us a movie. We would be very happy if he did that.

Who knows? I have no right to expect it, let’s put it that way. I don’t want to sound like a broken record, but I have such gratitude for the fact that this came along my path at this point. I have no right to expect anything like that to happen again. But in the meantime, I just want to make good stuff and definitely keep working with Ben, because we’re at that point in our life where we’re like, “Fuck it, why not?” This was our dream as kids, as boys. We got to honor those boys and keep working together if we can. ✦

#6764. A Brutally Honest Editor’s Guide to Amazon’s Early Black Friday iPad Deals

Good news for early birds: I’m already seeing $100 off the latest iPad Air.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

As a shopping editor who tracks prices on Apple products all year, I have high standards. Truth be told, Apple tech deals have had some highs and lows this year—some items reached their lowest prices ever during major sale moments like Prime Day, while others (I’m looking at you, Labor Day deals!) have been less than impressive. Now that Black Friday is mere weeks away, I’m creating an official guide to the iPad deals you should shop now....and which may become steeper soon.

When it comes to which iPad deals are actually worth your money, I’m forecasting steeper deals soon. Ahead, you’ll find an updated guide to the iPad deals I expect to be near their lowest prices ever leading up to Black Friday, and two deals that are already live today:

So far, the best pre-Black Friday iPad deal up for grabs is $100 off the 2026 iPad Air. The latest iPad Air model, it’s powered by the M4 chip, which boasts advanced graphics and a powerful processing system that multitasks with ease—whether you’re editing videos, jotting down notes, or creating presentation slides. When paired with the Magic Keyboard, it can function more like a laptop than a typical tablet. It’s available in 11-inch and 13-inch sizes, with storage ranging from 512GB to 1TB.

Price-wise, this isn’t the best discount I’ve ever seen. It’s been on sale for as low as $519, a whopping $130 cheaper than its current sale price. Even though it’s the steepest deal available right now, I’d almost bet money on the chance that it drops in price closer to Black Friday.

Another iPad deal I’m keeping an eye on? The 11th-generation iPad, which was released in 2025. Although its current $50 discount isn’t particularly impressive, I saw it drop below $300 during last year’s Black Friday sale. I recommend holding out just a little longer to get a better deal.

Compared to the iPad Air, this model sacrifices some processing power for a more affordable price point. The A16 chip is better suited for tasks like scrolling your TikTok feed, flipping through your e-book library, and streaming the Love Is Blind finale, so it’s a great option if you prefer using your iPad in recreational settings. It comes in three storage sizes—128, 256, and 512GB—with the best discount on the 128GB model.

All in all, the iPad deals available right now aren’t worth writing home about—but don’t despair! These deals can literally change in an instant, and I’m predicting that prices will become comparable to last year’s Black Friday sale if you’re willing to wait. Ahead, I’ve rounded up a few more iPad models worth keeping tabs on ahead of Black Friday.

#6763. Jesse Jo Stark on What It Means to Be a Muse

The singer’s new gothic country album, MUSE, releases on November 6

Jesse Jo Stark wrote her latest album MUSE in the wake of heartache. She left Los Angeles to find reprieve in the Colorado Rocky Mountains, where she found sanctuary among friends, a bottle of rosé, and a pink halter top and sweatpants combo. The melancholic album flowed out of her for three weeks, during which she transferred her emotions onto the page. “I am no longer holding on to a version of myself,” she tells Bazaar on a call from Los Angeles, on her way to a photoshoot. “There’s a freedom in moving forward.”

Stark keeps pressing on, no matter what. Outside of songwriting, she plays a key role in Chrome Hearts as the daughter of founder Richard Stark. She spent her formative years surrounded by musicians, artists, designers, and every kind of creative person imaginable, all of whom inspired her to pursue her own vision. That world endlessly inspires her to create, even when she feels low. “I’ve worn things that are absolutely horrible, and I’ve written songs that I don’t necessarily love,” she says, “but when you grow, you go back to a less constructed version of yourself.”

This more authentic version of herself fuels MUSE. She shed some of the macabre edge of her debut album 2022’s Doomed in favor of a softer reflection on her life, love, femininity, and the natural world that inspired these songs. But above all, Stark offers a reflection on her life being put on a pedestal, a narrative that deeply considers the women in her life, from friends to her mother, Laurie Lynn Stark. This 13-track gothic country album appropriately conveys Stark’s energy, with visuals described as “somewhat of a horror film, but I’m wearing a lace skirt.” She wrote on her Instagram, announcing the album, that the theme is “fragile but fierce femininity,” best to “enjoy with a glass of red.”

A few weeks before the release, Bazaar spoke to Stark about the women who inspired her, Napoleon’s poems to Josephine, writing songs in Colorado, and what it means to be a muse.

I was looking at a lot of mythology and history and the muses of rock and roll—women of rock and roll. If you break down the word itself, you get “used,” “amused,” “music.” I think women are admired and desired, but not always heard. For me, women have this pivotal point in their life—hopefully, at one point—where they regain their strength and voice, and this album was that for me. The muse, throughout history, could also have a negative connotation and feel lonely. But I felt like putting emphasis on the reciprocity of it—then owning that and allowing it to feel beautiful. It’s a powerful word. It felt fitting for this time in my life.

There’s so much serious crossover. Being able to develop the way I feel my best on stage and design what I wear enables me to embody that version of myself, to gather the strength to perform. Women like Daphne Guinness, Isabella Blow and Jane Birkin have really inspired me. I’ve always wanted to not just completely mirror something from the past, but recreate it as my own. I wear a loincloth on stage—it’s not that it hasn’t been done before, but the way I wear it or the way it’s designed, the accessories and tops that are paired with it... I do feel confident in me extending this already living thing, but making it my own.

When I was little, I always had an attitude about the two not going hand in hand. I think that was incorrect. They’re pretty much the same expression: one’s a vocal cord and playing guitar, and the other’s a boot that you walk in.

It’s weird. My last album was highly visual. There was a lot of fantasy involved, and this is very stripped back; [both] vocal production-wise and stylistically. I just did my first acoustic show, and I was wearing a vintage silk dress with a heel, and my hair was straight, and I had minimal makeup. Because there was such a rawness to it, especially where I recorded, which was amongst nature and very secluded. With this record, anytime I tried to put emphasis on creating a specific vibe, I rejected that.

The space and the quietness and the snow—I’ve never been one to be completely influenced by nature. I don’t love green or grass, but I love to sit in it and lie in it. I love being with animals and the snow and the sun. All these natural tones, I’m embracing the color brown like I haven’t before. It sounds so silly, but they represent a softness that I didn’t have for a while… The visuals have become somewhat of a horror film, but I’m wearing a lace skirt.

My main single from the record, “age of being honest,” has been visually stimulating and powerful. I can feel every emotion through that visual. It’s rawness—in the vocals and everything. “colorado” is the place that I literally went to and didn’t really know what was going to come from it. That set the tone for the rest of the record.

The shedding was happening as I was driving. The heartache was there, so it wasn’t difficult once I sat down to break through that human brick wall. I wanted to feel everything right away. I gotta be real, it was a white boa, a bottle of rosé, people I fuck with, the sun, and being in a really beautiful pink halter top and sweatpants half the time. I don’t think there was much going on except pain and not knowing where it was going and just sitting on the floor, living in a house with people for two weeks or a month. You can’t escape what you’re doing; I really leaned into that.

My dad had a lot to do with my love of music. I asked to play guitar at a young age. I was around a lot of rock and roll and glam—these really amazing artists who were way older than me. This desire to—and I was really shy—create something as beautiful as they had or stand as my parents could and make my own way. Music was always mine, so there was an independence to it. Finding yourself in writing, you don’t think you can write, and then you start writing poems. I loved Britney Spears. I loved the Spice Girls. But then my dad would play Merle Haggard, and he also let me listen to Christina Aguilera. I found punk rock when I was 13 because I fell in love with a punk boy. Then you somehow are, you’re mad at everything, and you found your voice in that way. It just started to become this whole thing, and then I started singing covers because I wasn’t ready to write. And then when you write, you really write your first song—to create something out of nothing is really funny because the fact that we think we get to do that is funny to me, but also so freeing and amazing. You never want to stop.

#6762. The Samsung Frame TV Is 25% Off Right Now

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Black Friday be damned. This is as good a deal as you’ll see.

Back in the old days of Black Friday shopping, you had to wait outside a big-box store for to score a TV on sale. You had to bring a tent, a friend, and prepare for some sort of physical altercation. Now, thanks to Amazon’s lead-up deals, you can just shop online. We’ve lost something, but there wasn’t much to lose honestly. Samsung’s talked-about Frame TV on sale, without the fistfight. Thanksgiving is still weeks away, and the 65-inch size of the standard 2026 model is already 25 percent off.

You may notice Amazon keeps the sale price hidden until you add the TV to your cart, where it still doesn’t show the original full price—but compare Amazon’s prices with those on Samsung’s website, and you’ll see this early deal is very legit.

The standard Frame TV is great, but honestly, it’s worth spending a bit extra on the 2026 Pro model, which offers premium picture quality and sound. “When you need a TV that decorates your wall like a work of art, The Frame Pro is everything,” writes associate commerce editor Bryn Gelbert. “A glare-proof screen, thin bezel, and partnerships with the Met, MoMA, and a few high-profile artist estates make it the art TV to beat.”

Samsung’s Wireless One Connect box is another key distinction between the standard and Pro models. “This is the real game changer when it comes to aesthetics,” Gelbert notes in his comparison of popular art TVs. “The box and TV communicate wirelessly, so you don’t have to worry about stowing cables out of sight (or in the wall) to get the true Frame experience of passing your TV off as art when your unsuspecting neighbor stops by.”

You might assume older versions of the Frame TV offer significantly better discounts—but as of writing, the 2026 model is only about a hundred bucks more than the 2025 model, so it’s worth upgrading your setup with the newest tech available.

Samsung The Frame Pro TV (2025 Model, 65”)

Will Frame TV prices drop further during Amazon’s official Black Friday sale? Only time will tell—but based on price history during previous sales like Prime Day, we think it’s safe to shop now. The Pro model is already at its lowest price ever, and the standard model is only $50 above its all-time low. Shop early, and you can watch Thanksgiving football in crisp, clear 4K instead of on that piece of crap you’ve got now.

Of note: Amazon doesn’t have any markdowns on the decorative bezels, which are sold separately. Stick with the TV for now, then check back closer to Black Friday and Cyber Monday to see if Samsung drops new deals on Frame TV accessories.
